    23	\title{\textbf{Group 1l hydrogen scavengers are relatively depleted beside plant roots across the
    24	hyperarid Rub' al-Khali}}
    25	\author{\todo{author list and affiliations}\\ \small King Abdullah University of Science and Technology}
    26	\date{}
    27	
    28	\begin{document}
    29	\maketitle
    30	\linenumbers
    31	
    32	\section*{Abstract}
    33	Desert soil bacteria can persist on atmospheric hydrogen and carbon monoxide, which they oxidise with
    34	high-affinity enzymes. How the genes for these enzymes are distributed within and between desert
    35	soils is known mainly from single-time transects and from studies that treat hydrogen oxidation as one
    36	function. We typed the [NiFe]-hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunits of 5{,}438
    37	species-level genomes from the hyperarid Rub' al-Khali and quantified each group as copies per genome
    38	equivalent in 546 shotgun metagenomes from 59 transect sites, sampled on up to five expeditions between
    39	2023 and 2025 at the surface, in bulk soil at 5--10~cm and beside plant roots at the same depth.
    40	Relative to bulk soil, root-adjacent soil carried 4.8-fold fewer group 1l hydrogenase copies per genome
    41	(106 paired site visits at 47 sites; lower in 89\%), 1.8 log$_2$ units more than the change in control
    42	hydrogenases and carbon monoxide dehydrogenases, whereas groups 1h and 2a and form-I carbon monoxide
    43	dehydrogenase changed no more than the controls. The relative depletion of group 1l scaled with
    44	extract DNA yield and arose almost entirely from turnover of genera, and genera carrying group 1l were
    45	depleted beside roots beyond what their predicted growth potential and phylum explained. Surface soil,
    46	in contrast, carried less of all four high-affinity groups than bulk soil. Across sites, group 1l in
    47	bulk soil was negatively associated with long-term precipitation, but this association was not robust
    48	to spatial blocking, whereas all four groups at the surface decreased with precipitation in every
    49	spatial sensitivity analysis. These patterns identify plant-associated relative exclusion of one
    50	hydrogen-scavenging guild as a replicated feature of hyperarid soil, which measurements of biomass,
    51	carbon and gas uptake can now test.
    52	
    53	% ============================================================================
    54	\section*{Introduction}
    55	Soil bacteria oxidise hydrogen and carbon monoxide at their trace atmospheric concentrations, and
    56	soil uptake is the largest sink of tropospheric hydrogen \citep{ehhalt2009h2cycle}. Hydrogen is
    57	oxidised at atmospheric concentrations by group 1h and 1l [NiFe]-hydrogenases
    58	\citep{greening2016genomic,ortiz2021antarctic} and also by some group 2a enzymes
    59	\citep{islam2020group2a,grinter2023structural}; carbon monoxide is oxidised by the form-I carbon
    60	monoxide dehydrogenase \citep{king2007coox,cordero2019atmospheric}. Genomes encoding these enzymes are
    61	widespread in soil \citep{bay2021trace}, the energy they yield sustains dormant cells
    62	\citep{greening2015persistence,cordero2019atmospheric}, and in deserts atmospheric oxidation can
    63	contribute to primary production \citep{ji2017atmospheric,ray2022atmospheric}. Arid regions weigh
    64	heavily in estimates of the soil hydrogen sink \citep{reji2025h2sink}, yet how the capacity for
    65	trace-gas oxidation is distributed within a desert remains poorly resolved.
    66	
    67	Three factors have been examined, each with a different design. Along aridity gradients sampled once,
    68	the abundance of high-affinity hydrogenases and cell-specific hydrogen oxidation increased towards the
    69	dry end in the Negev \citep{bay2021aridity}, and hydrogen oxidation per 16S rRNA gene copy was highest in
    70	the most arid zone of the Namib \citep{tribbia2026namib}. Hydration experiments follow a single soil:
    71	wetting desert soil decreased the relative abundance and expression of group 1l genes while stimulating
    72	hydrogen uptake \citep{jordaan2020gobi}, and in another desert soil group 1l transcription increased
    73	during hydration \citep{imminger2024resuscitation}. Plants, finally, concentrate organic carbon. In the
    74	Mu Us Desert, genes for hydrogen and carbon monoxide oxidation were less abundant under shrubs than in
    75	open soil, decreased with organic carbon and behaved as traits of oligotrophs whose uptake was suppressed
    76	by added carbon \citep{li2023fertile}; most hydrogenases typed in that study belonged to groups 1d and
    77	2a. Across many plant species, predicted maximal growth rate is a leading predictor of which bacteria
    78	are enriched in the rhizosphere \citep{lopez2023rhizosphere}. Two questions remain open in hot sand
    79	deserts, which are under-represented among genome-resolved surveys
    80	\citep{ortiz2021antarctic,ray2022atmospheric,ray2023mmbr,hutchinson2025antarctic}: whether plants act on
    81	all high-affinity groups or on particular ones, and whether such a pattern reflects the growth strategy
    82	of the carriers, their phylogeny or the enzyme they carry.
    83	
    84	We addressed these questions in the Rub' al-Khali, a hyperarid sand desert, using shotgun metagenomes
    85	from 59 sites along a 1{,}043-km transect, sampled on up to five expeditions at three positions: the
    86	surface, bulk soil at 5--10~cm and root-adjacent soil at the same depth. The depth-matched contrast
    87	removes depth as a difference between root-adjacent and bulk soil, and repeated visits allow spatial
    88	and within-site associations with precipitation to be estimated separately. We typed every hydrogenase
    89	and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit in a catalogue of 5{,}438 species representatives and
    90	quantified each group in reads, using non-high-affinity hydrogenases and carbon monoxide dehydrogenases
    91	as controls that share the same normaliser. The analysis was exploratory. Our initial expectations, that
    92	plants would enrich hydrogen scavengers and that wetter years would reduce them, were not supported, and
    93	the questions below were formulated after inspecting the same data: whether the depletion of
    94	hydrogen-scavenging capacity beside roots is specific to one enzyme group; whether it reflects biomass
    95	scaling, lineage turnover or the growth strategy of the carriers; how the surface differs from bulk
    96	soil; and how robust the association with long-term aridity is across sites and over time. We report
    97	effect sizes with site-level uncertainty and control the false discovery rate within declared families
    98	of tests.
    99	
   100	% ============================================================================
   101	\section*{Materials and methods}
   102	
   103	\subsection*{Sampling, sequencing and genome catalogue}
   104	Sampling, DNA extraction, sequencing and the genome catalogue are shared with companion papers on the
   105	same transect\todo{cite the data descriptor and the MAG catalogue paper}. Sixty sites along a 1{,}043~km
   106	west to east transect through the Saudi Rub' al-Khali were sampled on five expeditions (17--20 March
   107	2023, 14 July 2023, 17--21 February 2024, 19--21 August 2024 and 12--15 October 2025). At each site and
   108	visit, three co-located replicates were taken at three positions: surface soil (0~cm), bulk subsurface
   109	soil (5--10~cm) and soil from the root zone of the nearest plant at 5--10~cm, which we call
   110	root-adjacent because soil was not consistently attached to roots. Soil was frozen at $-20^\circ$C in the
   111	field and stored at $-80^\circ$C. DNA was extracted from 0.9--1.2~g of soil with the QIAGEN PowerLyzer
   112	PowerSoil or DNeasy PowerSoil Pro kit and sequenced on an Illumina NovaSeq 6000 (150-nt paired reads;
   113	101-nt reads for the fourth expedition). Reads were quality-filtered with fastp v1.0.1
   114	\citep{chen2018fastp}. Samples from two special-interest locations outside the repeated transect (sites
   115	62 and 64) and samples without site metadata were excluded, so the analysis covers sites 1--60.
   116	
   117	Each sample was assembled with MEGAHIT v1.2.9 \citep{li2015megahit} and binned with SemiBin2 v2.2.1
   118	\citep{pan2023semibin2}; bins were refined with DAS\_Tool v1.1.7 \citep{sieber2018dastool}, assessed with
   119	CheckM2 v1.1.0 \citep{chklovski2023checkm2}, and the 12{,}434 bins of at least medium quality
   120	(completeness $\geq 50\%$, contamination $\leq 10\%$) were dereplicated with dRep v3.6.2
   121	\citep{olm2017drep} at 95\% average nucleotide identity into 5{,}438 species representatives.
   122	Representatives were classified with GTDB-Tk v2.7.2 \citep{chaumeil2022gtdbtk} against GTDB release 232
   123	\citep{parks2022gtdb} and annotated with Bakta v1.12.0 \citep{schwengers2021bakta} and eggNOG-mapper
   124	v2.1.13 \citep{cantalapiedra2021eggnog}. Relative abundance of each representative per sample was
   125	computed with CoverM v0.7.0 \citep{aroney2025coverm} in genome mode. For 1{,}010 representatives that
   126	refinement emitted as contig subsets of a parent bin, annotations were taken from the parent bin and
   127	restricted to genes on contigs retained in the representative, matched by sequence; genes on removed
   128	contigs were not counted towards carriage or traits.
   129	
   130	\subsection*{Typing of hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunits}
   131	Proteins annotated with the [NiFe]-hydrogenase large subunit orthologue (KEGG K06281) and the carbon
   132	monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit orthologue (K03520) were extracted from the representatives. Each
   133	hydrogenase was assigned the class of its best DIAMOND blastp hit (\texttt{--ultra-sensitive}) in HydDB
   134	\citep{sondergaard2016hyddb,greening2016genomic}. We analyse groups 1h, 1l and 2a separately, because all
   135	three include enzymes that oxidise atmospheric hydrogen \citep{greening2016genomic,islam2020group2a}. Each
   136	CoxL protein was called form I when it carried the form-I active-site motif AYXCSFR and form II when it
   137	carried AYRGAGR \citep{king2007coox,martincuadrado2009coxl}; proteins in which the motif region was
   138	missing were called form I at more than 55\% identity and at least 90\% query coverage to the three
   139	form-I references of \citet{cordero2019atmospheric} and were otherwise unresolved. A representative
   140	carries a group when it encodes at least one retained protein of that group.
   141	
   142	To check the genomic context of typed genes, we took the ten genes on each side of every typed large
   143	subunit on the same contig from the Bakta annotation and scored the co-occurrence of the hydrogenase
   144	small subunit (K06282, K05927, K00437), hydrogenase maturation genes (K04651--K04656, K03605, K03192, or
   145	Bakta product names) and, for CoxL, the medium and small subunits (K03519, K03518) and the accessory genes
   146	\textit{coxDEFG}. Proportions are reported for loci with at least five genes on both sides; they describe
   147	neighbourhood co-occurrence, not gene order or operon structure.
   148	
   149	For display, hydrogenase large subunits of at least 400 residues were clustered at 70\% identity with
   150	MMseqs2 v18.8cc5c \citep{steinegger2017mmseqs2}, combined with all HydDB group 1 and group 2 references,
   151	aligned with MAFFT v7.526 \citep{katoh2013mafft} (\texttt{--auto}), stripped of columns with more than 50\%
   152	gaps and used to build a tree with FastTree v2.2.0 \citep{price2010fasttree} (\texttt{-lg -gamma}), rooted
   153	on the clade that best separates the group 2 references. CoxL proteins of at least 600 residues were
   154	treated in the same way with the form-I references.
   155	
   156	\subsection*{Read-based quantification}
   157	Groups were quantified in reads so that sequences outside the genome catalogue contribute. A competitive
   158	protein database contained every typed hydrogenase large subunit of the representatives labelled with its
   159	HydDB class, all HydDB reference hydrogenases, every CoxL protein of the representatives labelled form I,
   160	form II or unresolved, the three form-I CoxL references, and 14 universal single-copy ribosomal proteins of
   161	the representatives (KEGG K02886, K02906, K02926, K02931, K02933, K02867, K02874, K02878, K02982, K02988,
   162	K02994, K02946, K02948 and K02965) clustered at 90\% identity. Every read is assigned to its single best
   163	hit, so related enzymes of other classes compete for each read. From each sample, 10 million forward reads
   164	were drawn with seqtk v1.5 \citep{li2023seqtk} (seed 11) and truncated to 100~nt so that the 101-nt and
   165	150-nt libraries were screened identically; reads shorter than 75~nt were discarded. Reads were searched
   166	with DIAMOND v2.0.15 \citep{buchfink2021diamond} blastx (\texttt{--id 50 --evalue 1e-5 --max-target-seqs
   167	1}), and each read's best hit with an alignment of at least 25 residues was counted at identity floors of
   168	50, 60 and 70\%. For each sample and label, reads were divided by the length of the hit subject in
   169	kilobases and summed, and each marker was divided by the median of the 14 ribosomal-protein values. The
   170	result, genome equivalents (GE), is the number of marker copies per genome in the sequenced DNA. GE are
   171	relative to the genomes present and do not measure abundance per gram of soil. The primary measure used the
   172	60\% identity floor and samples in which at least 10 of the 14 ribosomal proteins were detected.
   173	
   174	We report six markers: group 1l, 1h and 2a hydrogenases, form-I CoxL, all other [NiFe]-hydrogenases and
   175	all other CoxL (form II and unresolved). The last two serve as controls. Of the 573 sequenced samples
   176	screened, 546 from sites 1--60 had complete metadata and passed the ribosomal-protein filter (116
   177	surface, 164 bulk and 266 root-adjacent samples; median 9.77 million reads screened).
   178	
   179	To estimate how well reads are assigned when their source is absent from the database, we tiled 100-nt
   180	fragments every 30~nt over each typed gene and held out, in five folds each, either all marker proteins of
   181	20\% of the representatives or 20\% of the clusters of marker proteins at 50\% identity, so that no
   182	remaining database protein shared 50\% identity with the source. Fragments of 50 random non-marker genes
   183	per held-out genome were added as negatives, and fragments were classified exactly as the reads.
   184	
   185	\subsection*{Contamination screen}
   186	Representatives whose GTDB genus matched a 16S rRNA amplicon variant called as a contaminant in 17
   187	extraction blanks of the fifth expedition were flagged, and a re-implementation of the frequency method of
   188	decontam \citep{davis2018decontam} compared abundance models independent of and inversely proportional to
   189	extracted DNA concentration, both over all samples with a single recorded concentration and after centring
   190	within each combination of expedition and extraction kit. Reads whose best hit belonged to a flagged
   191	representative were removed in a sensitivity analysis.
   192	
   193	\subsection*{Environmental covariates}
   194	Daily precipitation for 1995--2025 was obtained from the ERA5 reanalysis \citep{hersbach2020era5} at
   195	0.25$^\circ$ resolution through the Open-Meteo archive (\texttt{models=era5}). Long-term mean annual
   196	precipitation was computed for 1995--2024 and, as sensitivity, from the NASA POWER 2001--2020 climatology
   197	based on MERRA-2 \citep{gelaro2017merra2} and from Open-Meteo's default blend of ERA5 with the ECMWF
   198	operational model. Mean annual precipitation from ERA5 ranged from 13 to 19~mm across sites; because
   199	reanalysis precipitation in Saudi Arabia agrees only moderately with gauges \citep{wang2025sara}, we
   200	treat it as a ranking of sites rather than an absolute water balance. Antecedent precipitation was the ERA5
   201	sum over the 90 and 365 days before each site visit. Vegetation greenness was the median MODIS normalised
   202	difference vegetation index (NDVI; MOD13Q1 and MYD13Q1 v061, 250~m \citep{didan2021mod13q1}) over the 60
   203	days before sampling. Extract DNA concentration was taken from the sample ledger for samples with a single
   204	positive recorded value (267 of 546 samples). Soil geochemistry was summarised from X-ray fluorescence
   205	measurements of the same sites by centred log-ratio principal components and by Ca, Cl and S. Soil organic
   206	carbon and water content were not measured on these samples.
   207	
   208	\subsection*{Statistical analysis}
   209	Analyses were run in R v4.4.3 with lme4 v2.0.6 \citep{bates2015lme4}, vegan v2.7.5
   210	\citep{oksanen2025vegan} and ape v5.8.1 \citep{paradis2019ape}. A site visit is one site sampled on one
   211	expedition. For paired contrasts between positions, each marker was summarised as the median over
   212	replicates within a site visit and the log$_2$ ratio of the medians computed for each visit with both
   213	positions, with a pseudocount of 0.01~GE (results without a pseudocount and with 0.001 and 0.1 are
   214	reported). Confidence intervals were obtained by resampling sites with replacement (2{,}000 resamples), and
   215	tests are Wilcoxon signed-rank tests on site means of the visit values, so that the site is the
   216	independent unit. The excess change of a high-affinity marker is its log$_2$ ratio minus the mean of the
   217	two control log$_2$ ratios. A shared change in the ribosomal-protein denominator would shift all markers
   218	equally, and the excess removes it up to the effects of the pseudocount and of taking replicate medians
   219	separately for each marker. Mixed models of log$_2$ GE with marker, position, their interaction and
   220	expedition as fixed effects and site, site visit and sample as random intercepts tested the interaction
   221	by likelihood ratio. The contribution of position to each marker was summarised as the gain in marginal
   222	$R^2$ when position is added to a model with expedition.
   223	
   224	To relate group 1l to biomass, log$_2$ GE was regressed on log$_2$ extract DNA concentration with position,
   225	expedition and extraction kit as fixed effects and site and site visit as random intercepts; the DNA term
   226	was split into within-visit and between-visit components, the model was repeated within visits that used a
   227	single kit and within bulk soil, and attenuation for a reliability of DNA concentration as a biomass proxy
   228	of 0.8 and 0.6 was computed. Under a model in which group 1l copies per gram scale with biomass with
   229	exponent $\alpha$, GE scale with exponent $\alpha-1$. The root effect on group 1l was estimated with and
   230	without the DNA term.
   231	
   232	The change in the share of the mapped community held by group 1l carriers between root-adjacent and bulk
   233	soil was decomposed into a genus-turnover term (change in genus abundance at the mean carrier fraction of
   234	each genus) and a within-genus term (change in the carrier fraction of each genus at its mean abundance).
   235	The same decomposition was applied across bulk-soil sites against mean annual precipitation, and within
   236	the 81 genera that contain both carriers and non-carriers, the carrier fraction was modelled on
   237	precipitation in a binomial mixed model. To separate the number of carriers from the number of copies per
   238	carrier, the root contrast was computed for the abundance share of carriers ($F$), their copy-weighted share
   239	($M$) and copies per carrier ($C$).
   240	
   241	For growth strategy, the minimum doubling time of each representative was predicted from codon usage bias
   242	with gRodon2 v2.7.3 \citep{weissman2021grodon} in partial mode from coding sequences of at least 240~nt
   243	with ribosomal-protein genes as the highly expressed set; these are predictions of maximal growth
   244	potential, not realised growth. Doubling time, completeness-adjusted genome size and GC content were
   245	compared between carriers and non-carriers in mixed models with order as a random intercept. The
   246	community-weighted mean log doubling time of a sample was the CoverM-weighted mean over representatives
   247	with an estimate, and its shift between root-adjacent and bulk soil was computed per site visit. The
   248	excess change of group 1l was regressed on this shift with site and expedition as random intercepts, and
   249	the intercept, which estimates the excess at zero shift, was bootstrapped over sites. Across genera with a
   250	mean bulk-soil share of at least 0.05\% of the mapped community, the median visit-level log$_2$ ratio
   251	between root-adjacent and bulk soil was regressed on median log doubling time and the fractions of
   252	representatives carrying groups 1l, 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL, with phylum as a fixed effect and weights
   253	equal to the square root of the bulk-soil share; the model was repeated for genera with a nonzero ratio and
   254	for genera with at least two representatives. Co-carriage of the Calvin--Benson--Bassham cycle (RuBisCO
   255	large and small subunits and phosphoribulokinase) was compared between group 1l and 1h carriers in a
   256	binomial mixed model with order as a random intercept, with and without genome completeness.
   257	
   258	Spatial associations were Spearman correlations of site medians with mean annual precipitation for each
   259	position and marker, with confidence intervals from a bootstrap over $k$-means spatial blocks of the site
   260	coordinates ($k = 4, 6, 8, 10$), Moran's $I$ of the residuals with inverse-distance weights, and partial
   261	correlations controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation, for the first two geochemical principal
   262	components, for Ca, Cl and S, and for the first principal coordinate of community composition. A mixed
   263	model over all samples regressed log$_2$ GE on standardised log precipitation with position and expedition
   264	as fixed effects and site and site visit as random intercepts. For change over time, log antecedent
   265	precipitation was split into a within-site component and a site mean, and log$_2$ visit medians were
   266	modelled on both with expedition as a fixed effect and site as a random intercept; the within-site slope
   267	was bootstrapped over sites and scaled to the within-site interquartile range of precipitation, and an
   268	association was called equivalent to zero when both bootstrap limits of that scaled change lay within
   269	$\pm 0.5$ log$_2$ units. Turnover of composition was partitioned by PERMANOVA
   270	\citep{anderson2001permanova} on Bray--Curtis dissimilarities with expedition permuted within site.
   271	The Benjamini--Hochberg procedure \citep{benjamini1995fdr} was applied within each declared family:
   272	position contrasts, spatial correlations, within-site temporal slopes and exposure models of the surface.
   273	
   274	Sensitivity variants recomputed the position contrasts and spatial correlations at identity floors of 50
   275	and 70\%, without reads to contamination-flagged representatives, without reads to the 122 group 1l
   276	proteins typed below 50\% identity or 80\% coverage to their HydDB hit, without the fifth and without the
   277	fourth expedition.
   278	
   279	% ============================================================================
   280	\section*{Results}
   281	
   282	\subsection*{Group 1l is the most abundant high-affinity hydrogenase in Rub' al-Khali soil}
   283	Of the 5{,}438 species representatives, 597 encode a group 1l, 410 a group 1h and
   284	134 a group 2a hydrogenase, and 245 a form-I CoxL (Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}a,
   285	Fig.~\ref{fig:coxl}). In loci with at least five genes on each side, a small-subunit gene co-occurred
   286	with 73\% of group 1l, 84\% of group 1h and all group 2a large subunits, and maturation genes with 88\%,
   287	96\% and 100\%; the medium and small CoxL subunits co-occurred with 96\% of form-I CoxL. In bulk soil, the
   288	median sample carried 1.08 group 1l copies per genome equivalent, 0.14 group 1h, 0.03 group 2a and 0.11
   289	form-I CoxL. The read classifier was specific for these groups when the source genes were withheld. With
   290	all proteins sharing 50\% identity with the source removed from the database, 89\% of group 1l fragments
   291	were assigned to group 1l and 0.7\% to group 1h, 99\% of group 1h fragments to group 1h, 94\% of group 2a
   292	fragments to group 2a and 82\% of form-I CoxL fragments to form I; of 1.5 million fragments from
   293	non-marker genes, two were assigned to group 1l (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark}).
   294	
   295	\subsection*{Root-adjacent soil carries less group 1l relative to other genes}
   296	We compared root-adjacent and bulk soil in the 106 site visits at 47 sites where both were sampled at
   297	5--10~cm. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots by a median of 2.27 log$_2$ units, a 4.8-fold difference (95\%
   298	CI $-2.90$ to $-1.59$; lower in 89\% of visits; $p=3\times10^{-7}$; Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}b). The control
   299	hydrogenases and CoxL were lower by 0.40 and 0.28 units, and the excess change of group 1l beyond the
   300	controls was $-1.83$ ($-2.62$ to $-1.00$; $q=2\times10^{-6}$). Groups 1h ($-0.01$), 2a ($-0.02$) and form-I
   301	CoxL ($-0.05$) changed no more than the controls (all $q>0.8$; Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}d). In the mixed model,
   302	marker and position interacted ($\chi^2=324$, 5 df), and only the group 1l interaction departed from the
   303	reference ($-1.75$, SE 0.12; other high-affinity groups between $-0.08$ and $+0.07$). Position accounted for
   304	a gain in marginal $R^2$ of 0.22 for group 1l and 0.02--0.10 for the other markers.
   305	
   306	The difference was consistent across expeditions, analysis choices and lineages. The excess change of group
   307	1l was negative in each expedition ($-0.99$ to $-2.63$), between $-1.54$ and $-1.85$ in every sensitivity
   308	variant (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}), and $-1.82$ without a pseudocount and $-1.32$ with a pseudocount of
   309	0.1. All ten orders of group 1l carriers with the highest share of bulk soil had a lower share beside roots
   310	(Table~\ref{tab:lineages}).
   311	
   312	\subsection*{The depletion of group 1l scales with DNA yield and with genus turnover}
   313	Copies per genome equivalent fall either when carriers decline or when other organisms increase. Extract
   314	DNA concentration, a proxy that combines microbial biomass with extraction efficiency and plant DNA, was a
   315	median 10-fold higher in root-adjacent than in bulk soil (log$_2$ ratio 3.37, 47 visits). Across 267
   316	samples, group 1l GE fell by 0.32 log$_2$ units (SE 0.03) per doubling of DNA concentration, within visits
   317	($-0.30$) as well as between them ($-0.35$), within visits extracted with a single kit ($-0.36$) and within
   318	bulk soil alone ($-0.23$), whereas groups 1h and 2a ($-0.02$ and $-0.03$) and the controls did not scale with
   319	DNA (Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}a). Under the scaling model this slope corresponds to an exponent of about 0.7:
   320	if DNA concentration tracked biomass, group 1l copies per gram would increase less than proportionally
   321	with biomass. Adjusting for DNA concentration reduced the root effect on group 1l from $-2.12$ (SE 0.23) to
   322	$-1.36$ (SE 0.20; Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}c). Because DNA concentration is not a calibrated measure of
   323	microbial biomass, these data establish a relative depletion of group 1l and a sub-proportional scaling
   324	with DNA yield, not a loss of group 1l carriers per gram of soil.
   325	
   326	The change was carried by lineage composition. The share of the mapped community held by group 1l
   327	carriers fell from a median of 0.31 in bulk soil to 0.06 beside roots, and genus turnover
   328	accounted for 97\% of this change; the carrier fraction within genera changed little
   329	($-0.005$). The share of carriers fell by 2.17 log$_2$ units, their copy-weighted share by 2.30 and
   330	the number of copies per carrier by only 0.20, so the depletion reflects fewer carrier genomes rather than
   331	fewer copies in each.
   332	
   333	\subsection*{Genera carrying group 1l are depleted beyond their predicted growth potential}
   334	Root-adjacent communities had shorter predicted doubling times than bulk soil in 79\% of 106 visits
   335	(community-weighted log doubling time $-0.26$; $p=1.4\times10^{-5}$), consistent with the enrichment of
   336	fast-growing taxa in rhizospheres \citep{lopez2023rhizosphere}. Within orders, carriers of group 1l
   337	($+0.12$, SE 0.03), group 1h ($+0.12$) and group 2a ($+0.20$) had longer predicted
   338	doubling times than non-carriers, so a shift towards faster-growing organisms should deplete all three
   339	groups. Only group 1l, however, was depleted beyond the controls. The excess change of group 1l was larger
   340	where the community shifted more ($\rho=$0.26; slope 1.73, SE 0.61), but at zero shift
   341	the model still predicted an excess of $-1.57$ (site bootstrap $-2.22$ to $-0.93$;
   342	Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}c). Across 289 genera, the fraction of a genus carrying group 1l predicted its
   343	depletion beside roots ($-0.81$ log$_2$ units, SE 0.18) with predicted doubling time, carriage
   344	of the other groups and phylum in the model, whereas carriage of groups 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL did not
   345	(Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}b). The association held among genera with a nonzero ratio ($-0.92$) and among
   346	genera with at least two representatives ($-1.08$). Vegetation greenness was negatively associated
   347	with group 1l in bulk soil ($-0.80$ log$_2$ units per standard deviation of NDVI, SE 0.19) and at the surface
   348	($-1.17$, SE 0.16) with mean annual precipitation in the model, and the excess change beside roots was
   349	smaller at greener sites (site medians, $\rho=0.37$, 47 sites).
   350	
   351	Group 1l carriers also differed in genome traits. Within orders, they had higher GC content than
   352	non-carriers (+0.7 percentage points) and genomes of similar size, whereas group 1h, group 2a and
   353	form-I CoxL carriers had larger genomes (+7\%, +11\% and +17\%). Calvin--Benson--Bassham genes were
   354	encoded by 18\% of group 1l-only and 8\% of group 1h-only carriers, a difference that was not
   355	significant within orders (log-odds 0.73, SE 0.39, $p=0.06$) but was after adjustment for genome
   356	completeness (0.96, $p=0.02$).
   357	
   358	\subsection*{The surface carries less of all high-affinity groups than bulk soil}
   359	The surface contrast differed in kind from the root contrast. In 68 visits at 36 sites, all four
   360	high-affinity groups were lower at the surface than in bulk soil beyond the controls: group 1l by 0.83
   361	log$_2$ units, group 1h by 0.42, group 2a by 0.22 and form-I CoxL by 0.24 (all $q<0.02$;
   362	Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}c,d). Root-adjacent soil therefore differed from the surface by a lower excess of group
   363	1l ($-0.85$) and a higher excess of group 1h ($+0.41$; 84 visits). At the surface, the excess change of
   364	group 1h increased with air temperature over the preceding 30 days (1.76 log$_2$ units per standard
   365	deviation, $q=0.001$), while associations with soil moisture and antecedent precipitation were not
   366	significant after correction.
   367	
   368	\subsection*{Association with long-term aridity is robust at the surface but not in bulk soil}
   369	Across sites, group 1l GE in bulk soil were negatively associated with ERA5 mean annual precipitation
   370	($\rho=-0.37$, $p=0.007$, 52 sites), with NASA POWER ($-0.42$) and with the blended product ($-0.53$), and
   371	residual spatial autocorrelation was weak (Moran's $I$ 0.03, $p=0.30$). The association held after
   372	controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation ($-0.33$) and for Ca, Cl and S ($-0.35$), but its spatial
   373	block bootstrap intervals included zero for every number of blocks (for six blocks $-0.65$ to $0.16$;
   374	Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}d), and controlling for the first principal coordinate of community composition
   375	removed it ($0.01$). Groups 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL in bulk soil were not associated with precipitation
   376	after correction. At the surface, all four groups were negatively associated with precipitation
   377	($\rho$ between $-0.48$ and $-0.54$, all $q<0.002$, 46 sites), with block bootstrap intervals that excluded
   378	zero and with partial correlations that persisted after controlling for geography and geochemistry; residual
   379	spatial autocorrelation remained for group 1l (Moran's $I$ 0.16, $p<0.001$) but not for the other groups. In
   380	root-adjacent soil, only group 2a was associated ($\rho=-0.42$). Over all samples, a mixed model gave $-0.48$
   381	log$_2$ units per standard deviation of log precipitation for group 1l (SE 0.11) and between $-0.10$ and
   382	$-0.32$ for the other groups.
   383	
   384	The bulk-soil association, where present, again ran through lineage composition. Holding the carrier
   385	fraction of each genus at its mean reproduced the observed correlation of the carrier share with
   386	precipitation ($-0.37$ against $-0.38$), although within the 81 genera containing both
   387	carriers and non-carriers the carrier fraction also decreased with precipitation ($-0.29$ per standard
   388	deviation, SE 0.12).
   389	
   390	\subsection*{Within-site change between expeditions}
   391	Antecedent precipitation is now available for all five expeditions, covering 31 months. Within sites, after
   392	accounting for expedition, change in bulk-soil group 1l GE was not significantly associated with
   393	precipitation over the preceding year (103 visits at 35 sites; $q=0.34$) or 90 days, and the confidence
   394	intervals were too wide to exclude changes of 0.5 log$_2$ units across the within-site range of
   395	precipitation (Fig.~\ref{fig:temporal}). At the surface and beside roots, group 1l was lower after wetter
   396	90-day periods (changes of $-0.65$ and $-0.73$ log$_2$ units across the within-site interquartile range;
   397	$q=0.013$ and $0.020$), whereas changes in groups 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL in bulk and root-adjacent soil
   398	over 90 days were within the equivalence bound. Expedition
   399	explained 2.0\% of the variation in community composition with expedition permuted within site ($p=0.001$),
   400	site 28.8\% and position 4.6\%, and dispersion differed among expeditions ($p=0.012$). These data show that
   401	composition changed measurably between expeditions but do not determine whether group 1l in bulk soil
   402	tracks interannual rainfall.
   403	
   404	% ============================================================================
   405	\section*{Discussion}
   406	Across 47 sites and five expeditions, root-adjacent soil carried a smaller share of group 1l hydrogenase
   407	genes than bulk soil at the same depth, and this was the only high-affinity group whose share fell more than
   408	that of control genes. The depletion scaled with extract DNA yield, was carried by turnover of genera rather
   409	than by changes within genera, and was associated with carrying group 1l beyond predicted growth potential
   410	and phylum. The surface showed a different pattern, with all high-affinity groups depleted, and only the
   411	surface pattern was robustly associated with long-term aridity.
   412	
   413	The closest precedent is the depletion of trace-gas oxidation genes under shrubs in the Mu Us Desert
   414	\citep{li2023fertile}. Li et al. measured organic carbon, carbon additions and uptake and attributed the
   415	depletion to oligotrophic oxidisers being outcompeted where carbon is available; most of their hydrogenases
   416	were of groups 1d and 2a. Our data add a group-resolved and replicated contrast: in the Rub' al-Khali, the
   417	plant-associated depletion was confined to group 1l, while groups 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL changed no more
   418	than control genes. Predicted growth potential is a dominant predictor of rhizosphere enrichment
   419	\citep{lopez2023rhizosphere}, and root-adjacent communities here were faster-growing, but carriers of groups
   420	1l, 1h and 2a all had long predicted doubling times, and only group 1l was depleted beyond the controls.
   421	Something that distinguishes group 1l carriers, or the habitats they occupy, is therefore needed to explain
   422	the pattern. Group 1l carriers are mostly Actinomycetota (59\%) and Chloroflexota (15\%) with high GC content, and the
   423	depletion was carried by whole genera, so the enzyme may mark a lineage ecology rather than be the target of
   424	selection. Moisture and oxygen are other candidates. Hydration decreased the relative abundance and
   425	transcription of group 1l in one desert soil \citep{jordaan2020gobi} but increased group 1l transcription in
   426	another \citep{imminger2024resuscitation}, and both studies found hydrogen uptake stimulated by water, so
   427	the direction of any moisture effect on group 1l carriers is unresolved.
   428	
   429	The scaling with DNA yield constrains what the depletion means. A lower share of group 1l genes beside
   430	roots is expected if root-adjacent soil supports more biomass of other organisms while group 1l carriers
   431	increase less than proportionally, and DNA yield explained about a third of the root effect. Relative and
   432	quantitative profiles of rhizosphere communities can differ in direction \citep{azarbad2022relative}, so
   433	measurements of cells or gene copies per gram are needed before the depletion can be called a loss of
   434	carriers or of capacity. The genus-level association and the absence of any comparable depletion of groups
   435	1h and 2a are not explained by a shared change in biomass, which would affect all groups alike.
   436	
   437	The surface differs from both subsurface positions. All high-affinity groups were depleted relative to
   438	bulk soil, and all decreased towards wetter sites, consistently across precipitation products and spatial
   439	sensitivity analyses. The surface is exposed to the largest fluctuations of temperature, radiation and
   440	moisture, and the association of group 1h with recent temperature suggests that the surface filters
   441	trace-gas oxidisers by exposure. In bulk soil, by contrast, the negative association of group 1l with
   442	long-term precipitation extends the pattern of other aridity gradients \citep{bay2021aridity,tribbia2026namib}
   443	only weakly: it did not survive spatial blocking, and it disappeared when community composition was
   444	controlled for. Mean annual precipitation varied only between 13 and 19~mm across our sites by ERA5, and
   445	reanalysis precipitation in this region is uncertain \citep{wang2025sara}, so the transect samples a narrow
   446	and imprecisely measured part of the aridity range. Over 31 months, composition shifted measurably between
   447	expeditions, and at the surface and beside roots group 1l was lower after wetter 90-day periods, but within
   448	bulk soil the data neither show nor exclude a response of group 1l to interannual rainfall.
   449	
   450	The design measures the relative representation of genes in DNA. It does not measure activity, uptake or
   451	abundance per gram. DNA concentration combines microbial and plant DNA and extraction efficiency, and a
   452	calibrated measure of biomass, such as quantitative PCR of the 16S rRNA gene or a spike-in, would test
   453	whether group 1l carriers decline in absolute terms. Extracellular DNA can persist in dry soil
   454	\citep{carini2016relicdna,lennon2018relicdna}; the propidium-monoazide-treated amplicon pairs available for
   455	this transect come from two sites and include no bulk soil, so the contribution of relic DNA to the position
   456	contrasts is not known. NDVI at 250~m and predicted growth potential are indirect proxies for the carbon
   457	supply of a root microsite, and organic carbon and water content of root-adjacent and bulk soil were not
   458	measured. Root-adjacent and bulk soil also differ in moisture, oxygen, plant identity and microsite, which
   459	the depth-matched design does not separate. Finally, the questions were formulated after inspecting these
   460	data. The root contrast is replicated across sites, expeditions and analysis choices, but potential hydrogen
   461	uptake in archived root-adjacent and bulk soil from the same visits, together with calibrated biomass, would
   462	provide the independent test.
   463	
   464	% ============================================================================
   465	\section*{Data and code availability}
   466	The shotgun reads are deposited at the European Nucleotide Archive under study PRJEB125153 (public release
   467	scheduled for 1 November 2026)\todo{confirm release date and sample accessions}. The species representatives
   468	and their annotations are released with the companion catalogue paper. The analysis scripts, compact input
   469	and output tables, figure code and an R environment specification are at
   470	\url{https://github.com/bio-ontology-research-group/empty-quarter-mags} in the folder \texttt{lifeonair/};
   471	per-sample read counts are retrieved by the repository's large-file script, which checks them against a
   472	checksum manifest\todo{public archive with DOI before submission; the current fetch route requires
   473	institutional credentials}.

Three factors have been examined, each with a different design. Along aridity gradients sampled once,
the abundance of high-affinity hydrogenases and cell-specific hydrogen oxidation increased towards the
dry end in the Negev \citep{bay2021aridity}, and hydrogen oxidation per 16S rRNA gene copy was highest in
the most arid zone of the Namib \citep{tribbia2026namib}. Hydration experiments follow a single soil:
wetting desert soil decreased the relative abundance and expression of group 1l genes while stimulating
hydrogen uptake \citep{jordaan2020gobi}, and in another desert soil group 1l transcription increased
during hydration \citep{imminger2024resuscitation}. Plants, finally, concentrate organic carbon. In the
Mu Us Desert, genes for hydrogen and carbon monoxide oxidation were less abundant under shrubs than in
open soil, decreased with organic carbon and behaved as traits of oligotrophs whose uptake was suppressed
by added carbon \citep{li2023fertile}; most hydrogenases typed in that study belonged to groups 1d and
2a. Across many plant species, predicted maximal growth rate is a leading predictor of which bacteria
are enriched in the rhizosphere \citep{lopez2023rhizosphere}. Two questions remain open in hot sand
deserts, which are under-represented among genome-resolved surveys
\citep{ortiz2021antarctic,ray2022atmospheric,ray2023mmbr,hutchinson2025antarctic}: whether plants act on
all high-affinity groups or on particular ones, and whether such a pattern reflects the growth strategy
of the carriers, their phylogeny or the enzyme they carry.

We addressed these questions in the Rub' al-Khali, a hyperarid sand desert, using shotgun metagenomes
from 59 sites along a 1{,}043-km transect, sampled on up to five expeditions at three positions: the
surface, bulk soil at 5--10~cm and root-adjacent soil at the same depth. The depth-matched contrast
removes depth as a difference between root-adjacent and bulk soil, and repeated visits allow spatial
and within-site associations with precipitation to be estimated separately. We typed every hydrogenase
and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit in a catalogue of 5{,}438 species representatives and
quantified each group in reads, using non-high-affinity hydrogenases and carbon monoxide dehydrogenases
as controls that share the same normaliser. The analysis was exploratory. Our initial expectations, that
plants would enrich hydrogen scavengers and that wetter years would reduce them, were not supported, and
the questions below were formulated after inspecting the same data: whether the depletion of
hydrogen-scavenging capacity beside roots is specific to one enzyme group; whether it reflects biomass
scaling, lineage turnover or the growth strategy of the carriers; how the surface differs from bulk
soil; and how robust the association with long-term aridity is across sites and over time. We report
effect sizes with site-level uncertainty and control the false discovery rate within declared families
of tests.

% ============================================================================
\section*{Materials and methods}

\subsection*{Sampling, sequencing and genome catalogue}
Sampling, DNA extraction, sequencing and the genome catalogue are shared with companion papers on the
same transect\todo{cite the data descriptor and the MAG catalogue paper}. Sixty sites along a 1{,}043~km
west to east transect through the Saudi Rub' al-Khali were sampled on five expeditions (17--20 March
2023, 14 July 2023, 17--21 February 2024, 19--21 August 2024 and 12--15 October 2025). At each site and
visit, three co-located replicates were taken at three positions: surface soil (0~cm), bulk subsurface
soil (5--10~cm) and soil from the root zone of the nearest plant at 5--10~cm, which we call
root-adjacent because soil was not consistently attached to roots. Soil was frozen at $-20^\circ$C in the
field and stored at $-80^\circ$C. DNA was extracted from 0.9--1.2~g of soil with the QIAGEN PowerLyzer
PowerSoil or DNeasy PowerSoil Pro kit and sequenced on an Illumina NovaSeq 6000 (150-nt paired reads;
101-nt reads for the fourth expedition). Reads were quality-filtered with fastp v1.0.1
\citep{chen2018fastp}. Samples from two special-interest locations outside the repeated transect (sites
62 and 64) and samples without site metadata were excluded, so the analysis covers sites 1--60.

Each sample was assembled with MEGAHIT v1.2.9 \citep{li2015megahit} and binned with SemiBin2 v2.2.1
\citep{pan2023semibin2}; bins were refined with DAS\_Tool v1.1.7 \citep{sieber2018dastool}, assessed with
CheckM2 v1.1.0 \citep{chklovski2023checkm2}, and the 12{,}434 bins of at least medium quality
(completeness $\geq 50\%$, contamination $\leq 10\%$) were dereplicated with dRep v3.6.2
\citep{olm2017drep} at 95\% average nucleotide identity into 5{,}438 species representatives.
Representatives were classified with GTDB-Tk v2.7.2 \citep{chaumeil2022gtdbtk} against GTDB release 232
\citep{parks2022gtdb} and annotated with Bakta v1.12.0 \citep{schwengers2021bakta} and eggNOG-mapper
v2.1.13 \citep{cantalapiedra2021eggnog}. Relative abundance of each representative per sample was
computed with CoverM v0.7.0 \citep{aroney2025coverm} in genome mode. For 1{,}010 representatives that
refinement emitted as contig subsets of a parent bin, annotations were taken from the parent bin and
restricted to genes on contigs retained in the representative, matched by sequence; genes on removed
contigs were not counted towards carriage or traits.

\subsection*{Typing of hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunits}
Proteins annotated with the [NiFe]-hydrogenase large subunit orthologue (KEGG K06281) and the carbon
monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit orthologue (K03520) were extracted from the representatives. Each
hydrogenase was assigned the class of its best DIAMOND blastp hit (\texttt{--ultra-sensitive}) in HydDB
\citep{sondergaard2016hyddb,greening2016genomic}. We analyse groups 1h, 1l and 2a separately, because all
three include enzymes that oxidise atmospheric hydrogen \citep{greening2016genomic,islam2020group2a}. Each
CoxL protein was called form I when it carried the form-I active-site motif AYXCSFR and form II when it
carried AYRGAGR \citep{king2007coox,martincuadrado2009coxl}; proteins in which the motif region was
missing were called form I at more than 55\% identity and at least 90\% query coverage to the three
form-I references of \citet{cordero2019atmospheric} and were otherwise unresolved. A representative
carries a group when it encodes at least one retained protein of that group.

To check the genomic context of typed genes, we took the ten genes on each side of every typed large
subunit on the same contig from the Bakta annotation and scored the co-occurrence of the hydrogenase
small subunit (K06282, K05927, K00437), hydrogenase maturation genes (K04651--K04656, K03605, K03192, or
Bakta product names) and, for CoxL, the medium and small subunits (K03519, K03518) and the accessory genes
\textit{coxDEFG}. Proportions are reported for loci with at least five genes on both sides; they describe
neighbourhood co-occurrence, not gene order or operon structure.

For display, hydrogenase large subunits of at least 400 residues were clustered at 70\% identity with
MMseqs2 v18.8cc5c \citep{steinegger2017mmseqs2}, combined with all HydDB group 1 and group 2 references,
aligned with MAFFT v7.526 \citep{katoh2013mafft} (\texttt{--auto}), stripped of columns with more than 50\%
gaps and used to build a tree with FastTree v2.2.0 \citep{price2010fasttree} (\texttt{-lg -gamma}), rooted
on the clade that best separates the group 2 references. CoxL proteins of at least 600 residues were
treated in the same way with the form-I references.

\subsection*{Read-based quantification}
Groups were quantified in reads so that sequences outside the genome catalogue contribute. A competitive
protein database contained every typed hydrogenase large subunit of the representatives labelled with its
HydDB class, all HydDB reference hydrogenases, every CoxL protein of the representatives labelled form I,
form II or unresolved, the three form-I CoxL references, and 14 universal single-copy ribosomal proteins of
the representatives (KEGG K02886, K02906, K02926, K02931, K02933, K02867, K02874, K02878, K02982, K02988,
K02994, K02946, K02948 and K02965) clustered at 90\% identity. Every read is assigned to its single best
hit, so related enzymes of other classes compete for each read. From each sample, 10 million forward reads
were drawn with seqtk v1.5 \citep{li2023seqtk} (seed 11) and truncated to 100~nt so that the 101-nt and
150-nt libraries were screened identically; reads shorter than 75~nt were discarded. Reads were searched
with DIAMOND v2.0.15 \citep{buchfink2021diamond} blastx (\texttt{--id 50 --evalue 1e-5 --max-target-seqs
1}), and each read's best hit with an alignment of at least 25 residues was counted at identity floors of
50, 60 and 70\%. For each sample and label, reads were divided by the length of the hit subject in
kilobases and summed, and each marker was divided by the median of the 14 ribosomal-protein values. The
result, genome equivalents (GE), is the number of marker copies per genome in the sequenced DNA. GE are
relative to the genomes present and do not measure abundance per gram of soil. The primary measure used the
60\% identity floor and samples in which at least 10 of the 14 ribosomal proteins were detected.

We report six markers: group 1l, 1h and 2a hydrogenases, form-I CoxL, all other [NiFe]-hydrogenases and
all other CoxL (form II and unresolved). The last two serve as controls. Of the 573 sequenced samples
screened, 546 from sites 1--60 had complete metadata and passed the ribosomal-protein filter (116
surface, 164 bulk and 266 root-adjacent samples; median 9.77 million reads screened).

To estimate how well reads are assigned when their source is absent from the database, we tiled 100-nt
fragments every 30~nt over each typed gene and held out, in five folds each, either all marker proteins of
20\% of the representatives or 20\% of the clusters of marker proteins at 50\% identity, so that no
remaining database protein shared 50\% identity with the source. Fragments of 50 random non-marker genes
per held-out genome were added as negatives, and fragments were classified exactly as the reads.

\subsection*{Contamination screen}
Representatives whose GTDB genus matched a 16S rRNA amplicon variant called as a contaminant in 17
extraction blanks of the fifth expedition were flagged, and a re-implementation of the frequency method of
decontam \citep{davis2018decontam} compared abundance models independent of and inversely proportional to
extracted DNA concentration, both over all samples with a single recorded concentration and after centring
within each combination of expedition and extraction kit. Reads whose best hit belonged to a flagged
representative were removed in a sensitivity analysis.

\subsection*{Environmental covariates}
Daily precipitation for 1995--2025 was obtained from the ERA5 reanalysis \citep{hersbach2020era5} at
0.25$^\circ$ resolution through the Open-Meteo archive (\texttt{models=era5}). Long-term mean annual
precipitation was computed for 1995--2024 and, as sensitivity, from the NASA POWER 2001--2020 climatology
based on MERRA-2 \citep{gelaro2017merra2} and from Open-Meteo's default blend of ERA5 with the ECMWF
operational model. Mean annual precipitation from ERA5 ranged from 13 to 19~mm across sites; because
reanalysis precipitation in Saudi Arabia agrees only moderately with gauges \citep{wang2025sara}, we
treat it as a ranking of sites rather than an absolute water balance. Antecedent precipitation was the ERA5
sum over the 90 and 365 days before each site visit. Vegetation greenness was the median MODIS normalised
difference vegetation index (NDVI; MOD13Q1 and MYD13Q1 v061, 250~m \citep{didan2021mod13q1}) over the 60
days before sampling. Extract DNA concentration was taken from the sample ledger for samples with a single
positive recorded value (267 of 546 samples). Soil geochemistry was summarised from X-ray fluorescence
measurements of the same sites by centred log-ratio principal components and by Ca, Cl and S. Soil organic
carbon and water content were not measured on these samples.

\subsection*{Statistical analysis}
Analyses were run in R v4.4.3 with lme4 v2.0.6 \citep{bates2015lme4}, vegan v2.7.5
\citep{oksanen2025vegan} and ape v5.8.1 \citep{paradis2019ape}. A site visit is one site sampled on one
expedition. For paired contrasts between positions, each marker was summarised as the median over
replicates within a site visit and the log$_2$ ratio of the medians computed for each visit with both
positions, with a pseudocount of 0.01~GE (results without a pseudocount and with 0.001 and 0.1 are
reported). Confidence intervals were obtained by resampling sites with replacement (2{,}000 resamples), and
tests are Wilcoxon signed-rank tests on site means of the visit values, so that the site is the
independent unit. The excess change of a high-affinity marker is its log$_2$ ratio minus the mean of the
two control log$_2$ ratios. A shared change in the ribosomal-protein denominator would shift all markers
equally, and the excess removes it up to the effects of the pseudocount and of taking replicate medians
separately for each marker. Mixed models of log$_2$ GE with marker, position, their interaction and
expedition as fixed effects and site, site visit and sample as random intercepts tested the interaction
by likelihood ratio. The contribution of position to each marker was summarised as the gain in marginal
$R^2$ when position is added to a model with expedition.

To relate group 1l to biomass, log$_2$ GE was regressed on log$_2$ extract DNA concentration with position,
expedition and extraction kit as fixed effects and site and site visit as random intercepts; the DNA term
was split into within-visit and between-visit components, the model was repeated within visits that used a
single kit and within bulk soil, and attenuation for a reliability of DNA concentration as a biomass proxy
of 0.8 and 0.6 was computed. Under a model in which group 1l copies per gram scale with biomass with
exponent $\alpha$, GE scale with exponent $\alpha-1$. The root effect on group 1l was estimated with and
without the DNA term.

The change in the share of the mapped community held by group 1l carriers between root-adjacent and bulk
soil was decomposed into a genus-turnover term (change in genus abundance at the mean carrier fraction of
each genus) and a within-genus term (change in the carrier fraction of each genus at its mean abundance).
The same decomposition was applied across bulk-soil sites against mean annual precipitation, and within
the 81 genera that contain both carriers and non-carriers, the carrier fraction was modelled on
precipitation in a binomial mixed model. To separate the number of carriers from the number of copies per
carrier, the root contrast was computed for the abundance share of carriers ($F$), their copy-weighted share
($M$) and copies per carrier ($C$).

For growth strategy, the minimum doubling time of each representative was predicted from codon usage bias
with gRodon2 v2.7.3 \citep{weissman2021grodon} in partial mode from coding sequences of at least 240~nt
with ribosomal-protein genes as the highly expressed set; these are predictions of maximal growth
potential, not realised growth. Doubling time, completeness-adjusted genome size and GC content were
compared between carriers and non-carriers in mixed models with order as a random intercept. The
community-weighted mean log doubling time of a sample was the CoverM-weighted mean over representatives
with an estimate, and its shift between root-adjacent and bulk soil was computed per site visit. The
excess change of group 1l was regressed on this shift with site and expedition as random intercepts, and
the intercept, which estimates the excess at zero shift, was bootstrapped over sites. Across genera with a
mean bulk-soil share of at least 0.05\% of the mapped community, the median visit-level log$_2$ ratio
between root-adjacent and bulk soil was regressed on median log doubling time and the fractions of
representatives carrying groups 1l, 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL, with phylum as a fixed effect and weights
equal to the square root of the bulk-soil share; the model was repeated for genera with a nonzero ratio and
for genera with at least two representatives. Co-carriage of the Calvin--Benson--Bassham cycle (RuBisCO
large and small subunits and phosphoribulokinase) was compared between group 1l and 1h carriers in a
binomial mixed model with order as a random intercept, with and without genome completeness.

Spatial associations were Spearman correlations of site medians with mean annual precipitation for each
position and marker, with confidence intervals from a bootstrap over $k$-means spatial blocks of the site
coordinates ($k = 4, 6, 8, 10$), Moran's $I$ of the residuals with inverse-distance weights, and partial
correlations controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation, for the first two geochemical principal
components, for Ca, Cl and S, and for the first principal coordinate of community composition. A mixed
model over all samples regressed log$_2$ GE on standardised log precipitation with position and expedition
as fixed effects and site and site visit as random intercepts. For change over time, log antecedent
precipitation was split into a within-site component and a site mean, and log$_2$ visit medians were
modelled on both with expedition as a fixed effect and site as a random intercept; the within-site slope
was bootstrapped over sites and scaled to the within-site interquartile range of precipitation, and an
association was called equivalent to zero when both bootstrap limits of that scaled change lay within
$\pm 0.5$ log$_2$ units. Turnover of composition was partitioned by PERMANOVA
\citep{anderson2001permanova} on Bray--Curtis dissimilarities with expedition permuted within site.
The Benjamini--Hochberg procedure \citep{benjamini1995fdr} was applied within each declared family:
position contrasts, spatial correlations, within-site temporal slopes and exposure models of the surface.

Sensitivity variants recomputed the position contrasts and spatial correlations at identity floors of 50
and 70\%, without reads to contamination-flagged representatives, without reads to the 122 group 1l
proteins typed below 50\% identity or 80\% coverage to their HydDB hit, without the fifth and without the
fourth expedition.

% ============================================================================
\section*{Results}

\subsection*{Group 1l is the most abundant high-affinity hydrogenase in Rub' al-Khali soil}
Of the 5{,}438 species representatives, 597 encode a group 1l, 410 a group 1h and
134 a group 2a hydrogenase, and 245 a form-I CoxL (Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}a,
Fig.~\ref{fig:coxl}). In loci with at least five genes on each side, a small-subunit gene co-occurred
with 73\% of group 1l, 84\% of group 1h and all group 2a large subunits, and maturation genes with 88\%,
96\% and 100\%; the medium and small CoxL subunits co-occurred with 96\% of form-I CoxL. In bulk soil, the
median sample carried 1.08 group 1l copies per genome equivalent, 0.14 group 1h, 0.03 group 2a and 0.11
form-I CoxL. The read classifier was specific for these groups when the source genes were withheld. With
all proteins sharing 50\% identity with the source removed from the database, 89\% of group 1l fragments
were assigned to group 1l and 0.7\% to group 1h, 99\% of group 1h fragments to group 1h, 94\% of group 2a
fragments to group 2a and 82\% of form-I CoxL fragments to form I; of 1.5 million fragments from
non-marker genes, two were assigned to group 1l (Table~\ref{tab:benchmark}).

\subsection*{Root-adjacent soil carries less group 1l relative to other genes}
We compared root-adjacent and bulk soil in the 106 site visits at 47 sites where both were sampled at
5--10~cm. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots by a median of 2.27 log$_2$ units, a 4.8-fold difference (95\%
CI $-2.90$ to $-1.59$; lower in 89\% of visits; $p=3\times10^{-7}$; Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}b). The control
hydrogenases and CoxL were lower by 0.40 and 0.28 units, and the excess change of group 1l beyond the
controls was $-1.83$ ($-2.62$ to $-1.00$; $q=2\times10^{-6}$). Groups 1h ($-0.01$), 2a ($-0.02$) and form-I
CoxL ($-0.05$) changed no more than the controls (all $q>0.8$; Fig.~\ref{fig:guilds}d). In the mixed model,
marker and position interacted ($\chi^2=324$, 5 df), and only the group 1l interaction departed from the
reference ($-1.75$, SE 0.12; other high-affinity groups between $-0.08$ and $+0.07$). Position accounted for
a gain in marginal $R^2$ of 0.22 for group 1l and 0.02--0.10 for the other markers.

The difference was consistent across expeditions, analysis choices and lineages. The excess change of group
1l was negative in each expedition ($-0.99$ to $-2.63$), between $-1.54$ and $-1.85$ in every sensitivity
variant (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}), and $-1.82$ without a pseudocount and $-1.32$ with a pseudocount of
0.1. All ten orders of group 1l carriers with the highest share of bulk soil had a lower share beside roots
(Table~\ref{tab:lineages}).

\subsection*{The depletion of group 1l scales with DNA yield and with genus turnover}
Copies per genome equivalent fall either when carriers decline or when other organisms increase. Extract
DNA concentration, a proxy that combines microbial biomass with extraction efficiency and plant DNA, was a
median 10-fold higher in root-adjacent than in bulk soil (log$_2$ ratio 3.37, 47 visits). Across 267
samples, group 1l GE fell by 0.32 log$_2$ units (SE 0.03) per doubling of DNA concentration, within visits
($-0.30$) as well as between them ($-0.35$), within visits extracted with a single kit ($-0.36$) and within
bulk soil alone ($-0.23$), whereas groups 1h and 2a ($-0.02$ and $-0.03$) and the controls did not scale with
DNA (Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}a). Under the scaling model this slope corresponds to an exponent of about 0.7:
if DNA concentration tracked biomass, group 1l copies per gram would increase less than proportionally
with biomass. Adjusting for DNA concentration reduced the root effect on group 1l from $-2.12$ (SE 0.23) to
$-1.36$ (SE 0.20; Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}c). Because DNA concentration is not a calibrated measure of
microbial biomass, these data establish a relative depletion of group 1l and a sub-proportional scaling
with DNA yield, not a loss of group 1l carriers per gram of soil.

The change was carried by lineage composition. The share of the mapped community held by group 1l
carriers fell from a median of 0.31 in bulk soil to 0.06 beside roots, and genus turnover
accounted for 97\% of this change; the carrier fraction within genera changed little
($-0.005$). The share of carriers fell by 2.17 log$_2$ units, their copy-weighted share by 2.30 and
the number of copies per carrier by only 0.20, so the depletion reflects fewer carrier genomes rather than
fewer copies in each.

\subsection*{Genera carrying group 1l are depleted beyond their predicted growth potential}
Root-adjacent communities had shorter predicted doubling times than bulk soil in 79\% of 106 visits
(community-weighted log doubling time $-0.26$; $p=1.4\times10^{-5}$), consistent with the enrichment of
fast-growing taxa in rhizospheres \citep{lopez2023rhizosphere}. Within orders, carriers of group 1l
($+0.12$, SE 0.03), group 1h ($+0.12$) and group 2a ($+0.20$) had longer predicted
doubling times than non-carriers, so a shift towards faster-growing organisms should deplete all three
groups. Only group 1l, however, was depleted beyond the controls. The excess change of group 1l was larger
where the community shifted more ($\rho=$0.26; slope 1.73, SE 0.61), but at zero shift
the model still predicted an excess of $-1.57$ (site bootstrap $-2.22$ to $-0.93$;
Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}c). Across 289 genera, the fraction of a genus carrying group 1l predicted its
depletion beside roots ($-0.81$ log$_2$ units, SE 0.18) with predicted doubling time, carriage
of the other groups and phylum in the model, whereas carriage of groups 1h and 2a and form-I CoxL did not
(Fig.~\ref{fig:scaling}b). The association held among genera with a nonzero ratio ($-0.92$) and among
genera with at least two representatives ($-1.08$). Vegetation greenness was negatively associated
with group 1l in bulk soil ($-0.80$ log$_2$ units per standard deviation of NDVI, SE 0.19) and at the surface
($-1.17$, SE 0.16) with mean annual precipitation in the model, and the excess change beside roots was
smaller at greener sites (site medians, $\rho=0.37$, 47 sites).

Group 1l carriers also differed in genome traits. Within orders, they had higher GC content than
non-carriers (+0.7 percentage points) and genomes of similar size, whereas group 1h, group 2a and
form-I CoxL carriers had larger genomes (+7\%, +11\% and +17\%). Calvin--Benson--Bassham genes were
encoded by 18\% of group 1l-only and 8\% of group 1h-only carriers, a difference that was not
significant within orders (log-odds 0.73, SE 0.39, $p=0.06$) but was after adjustment for genome
completeness (0.96, $p=0.02$).

# Round 1 literature digest (Gemini 3.8 Flash via agy, 8 search directions, 12 Oct 2026)

Candidates were proposed by Flash and every DOI was checked against Crossref (title match); 5 mismatched DOIs were corrected by hand and none of the retained DOIs is fabricated. **The one-line relevance notes are Flash summaries and are NOT verified**: check the local PDF/XML before relying on any claim. Local files are in `lifeonair/literature/<key>.pdf` or `<key>.fulltext.xml`; "no local copy" means paywalled (citation + DOI only).

Search prompts: `search/round1/prompts/`; raw outputs with Flash syntheses: `search/round1/out/`.

| alzahrani_2021_microbiome-studies-saudi-arabia | 10.3390/microorganisms9102021 | context | Identifies the Rub' al-Khali as one of the least characterized desert biomes in Saudi Arabia and highlights the absence of large-scale shotgun metagenomic surveys in the southern desert. | XML |

| lopez_2023_growth-rate-dominant-factor | 10.1038/s41396-023-01453-6 | challenges | Finds that predicted maximal growth rate is the primary driver of rhizosphere enrichment across global soil MAGs, directly challenging our finding that growth rate explains only a minor part of root-adjacent shifts. | PDF |

| azarbad_2022_relative-quantitative-rhizosphere-microbiome | 10.3389/fmicb.2021.798023 | challenges | Shows that per-cell relative profiling produces spurious apparent depletion of soil oligotrophs in the rhizosphere due to copiotroph biomass expansion, warning that 1l loss could be a compositional artifact. | PDF |
| dip_2025_potential-biofertilization-effect-accompanied | 10.7717/peerj.20019 | challenges | Observes that plant roots and elevated H2 exert surprisingly modest restructuring on overall rhizosphere community composition, challenging the notion that plant roots dramatically repress all hydrogenotrophs universally. | PDF |
| zhang_2026_divergent-organic-carbon-accrual | 10.1016/j.agee.2025.110075 | context | Shows that plant root inputs in hyperarid soils create localized carbon accrual zones that diverge strongly from surrounding desert sands, setting the edaphic contrast between root-adjacent and bulk soils. | no local copy |

| mapelli_2018_stage-development-modulates-rhizosphere | 10.1038/s41396-017-0026-4 | context | Shows that roots exert a potent selective rhizosphere effect that filters soil microbial assemblages even in extreme barren deserts, directly bearing on the marked depletion of group 1l scavengers in root-adjacent soils. | PDF |
| chiri_2021_termite-emissions-select-hydrogenotrophic | 10.1073/pnas.2102625118 | context | Shows that localized gas emission hotspots shift microbial communities away from high-affinity atmospheric scavengers toward lower-affinity enzymes, providing an analogue for root-adjacent microenvironments. | no local copy |
| he_2025_microbial-life-history-strategies | 10.1128/msystems.00178-25 | context | Demonstrates that carbon-poor soils favor oligotrophic genomic traits that are rapidly selected against upon organic carbon input, illuminating why root-adjacent soils lose specialized group 1l scavengers. | XML |
| eida_2018_desert-plant-bacteria-reveal | 10.1371/journal.pone.0208223 | context | Profiles rhizosphere and bulk sand communities of pioneer Arabian desert plants including Panicum turgidum, demonstrating root-associated filtering in hyperarid sands. | PDF |
| khan_2022_microbiome-variation-across-populations | 10.3389/fpls.2022.841217 | context | Characterizes native plant root-associated microbiomes in the hyperarid Rub' al-Khali (Empty Quarter), providing an essential botanical and regional baseline for root-adjacent microbial assembly in our study area. | PDF |
| zhang_2024_variation-root-associated-microbial | 10.3390/plants13172468 | context | Contrasts root-associated and bulk sand microbial assemblages in hyperarid shrubs including Calligonum, characterizing root compartment filtering in mobile desert dunes. | XML |
| hou_2025_ecological-traits-high-affinity | 10.1016/j.soilbio.2025.109831 | supports | Delineates the niche specialization of high-affinity hydrogen-oxidizing bacteria in soils, demonstrating that atmospheric scavengers depend strictly on oligotrophic, carbon-starved conditions and are repressed by elevated organic carbon availability. | no local copy |
| marasco_2022_plant-rhizosheathroot-niche-edaphic | 10.1038/s43705-022-00130-7 | supports | Illustrates that desert plant roots create hypercompetitive edaphic micro-oases dominated by copiotrophs and interference competition, providing an ecological mechanism for why specialized bulk-soil atmospheric scavengers like group 1l are displaced. | PDF |
| king_2002_impacts-plant-roots-cycling | 10.1046/j.1365-2486.2002.00545.x | supports | Finds that plant roots stimulate or maintain substantial soil CO oxidation despite root activity, explaining why root-adjacent soils retain form-I CoxL capacity while losing group 1l H2 scavengers. | no local copy |
| baril_2023_carbon-amendments-microcosms-induce | 10.1093/femsec/fiad159 | supports | Demonstrates experimentally that labile carbon additions repress high-affinity H2 oxidation activity and deplete high-affinity hydrogenase gene abundance, providing a direct mechanism for root-adjacent group 1l loss. | XML |
| qi_2026_intensifying-aridity-undermines-role | 10.1111/gcb.70903 | supports | Finds that intensifying aridity decouples plant productivity (NDVI) from soil microbial stability relationships, aligning with the manuscript finding that plant-carbon proxies explain only a minor part of functional variation. | no local copy |
| alhasani_2026_diversity-ecological-drivers-rhizospheric | 10.25259/jksus_1695_2025 | supports | Demonstrates that root systems of wild desert shrubs across Saudi Arabian arid reserves exert strong selective filtering on soil bacteria, providing regional rhizosphere baseline data. | PDF |

| wildbur_2026_carbon-monoxide-oxidizers-different | 10.1093/femsec/fiag062 | supports | Shows that form I CoxL-carrying taxa are taxonomically diverse and stably maintained across soil chronosequences in barren mineral substrates. | XML |
| xu_2025_carbon-monoxide-exposure-stimulates | 10.1101/2025.01.21.634059 | supports | Reveals broad taxonomic spread and stimulation of form-I CoxL carboxydotrophs across soil lineages, supporting why CO capacity is spread over more genera and retained in root soils. | PDF |
| liu_2023_metabolic-versatility-microbial-communities | 10.1128/aem.01072-23 | supports | Shows that coxL genes are substantially more abundant and taxonomically widespread than [NiFe]-hydrogenase genes in hyperarid playa soils, corroborating the decoupling and broader functional redundancy of CO over H2 scavenging. | no local copy |

| quiza_2014_land-influences-distribution-activity | 10.3389/fmicb.2014.00271 | supports | Demonstrates that soil atmospheric CO consumption kinetics are driven specifically by Type I coxL genotypes and can vary independently from general soil microbial biomass. | PDF |
| hernandez_2020_reconstructing-genomes-carbon-monoxide | 10.3390/microorganisms8121880 | supports | Confirms that Form I CoxL genes are distributed across phylogenetically divergent uncultivated lineages in arid soils, supporting functional redundancy of CO oxidation. | PDF |

## Cross-direction themes raised by Flash (to be checked, not established)

1. **Compositional artefact for H1**: relative (per-genome) profiling can show oligotroph "loss" when rhizosphere biomass expands (Azarbad 2022); growth rate is reported as a dominant predictor of the rhizosphere effect (Lopez et al. 2023 ISME J), which bears on the claim that growth rate explains only a minor part of the 1l loss.
2. **Group 1l physiology**: Imminger 2024 (Nat Commun) reportedly finds desert 1l expression responsive to hydration; Kropp 2026 (mSystems) catabolite repression of high-affinity hydrogenase by glycerol; Baril & Constant 2023 labile-carbon repression; de la Porte 2024 rhizosphere effect promoting gas-oxidation persistence (contrary direction).
3. **Space-for-time**: failure of substitution is predicted by theory when temporal variance is small relative to the spatial gradient (Blois 2013, Damgaard 2019, Perret 2024, Lovell 2023, Knapp 2026 aridity vs drought); legacy effects (Hawkes 2017, Canarini 2021). Risk that "space-for-time fails" is framed as more novel/strong than a 31-month design permits.
4. **H2 novelty**: broader taxonomic spread/redundancy of form-I CoxL than of high-affinity hydrogenases reported elsewhere (King 2007, Islam 2019, Liu 2023 Dalangtan, Latorre 2026, Wildbur 2026).
5. **Covariate quality**: ERA5 precipitation reportedly poor over hyperarid Saudi Arabia (Wang 2025, Nelli 2024).
6. **Capacity vs activity / relic DNA**: Buzzard 2022, Bertagni 2021, Horstmann 2024, Li 2026 eLife, Carini 2020, Blazewicz 2013, Rocca 2015; contamination reporting standards for low-biomass samples (Fierer 2025 Nat Microbiol).
