## Cross-direction themes raised by Flash (to be checked, not established)

1. **Compositional artefact for H1**: relative (per-genome) profiling can show oligotroph "loss" when rhizosphere biomass expands (Azarbad 2022); growth rate is reported as a dominant predictor of the rhizosphere effect (Lopez et al. 2023 ISME J), which bears on the claim that growth rate explains only a minor part of the 1l loss.
2. **Group 1l physiology**: Imminger 2024 (Nat Commun) reportedly finds desert 1l expression responsive to hydration; Kropp 2026 (mSystems) catabolite repression of high-affinity hydrogenase by glycerol; Baril & Constant 2023 labile-carbon repression; de la Porte 2024 rhizosphere effect promoting gas-oxidation persistence (contrary direction).
3. **Space-for-time**: failure of substitution is predicted by theory when temporal variance is small relative to the spatial gradient (Blois 2013, Damgaard 2019, Perret 2024, Lovell 2023, Knapp 2026 aridity vs drought); legacy effects (Hawkes 2017, Canarini 2021). Risk that "space-for-time fails" is framed as more novel/strong than a 31-month design permits.
4. **H2 novelty**: broader taxonomic spread/redundancy of form-I CoxL than of high-affinity hydrogenases reported elsewhere (King 2007, Islam 2019, Liu 2023 Dalangtan, Latorre 2026, Wildbur 2026).
5. **Covariate quality**: ERA5 precipitation reportedly poor over hyperarid Saudi Arabia (Wang 2025, Nelli 2024).
6. **Capacity vs activity / relic DNA**: Buzzard 2022, Bertagni 2021, Horstmann 2024, Li 2026 eLife, Carini 2020, Blazewicz 2013, Rocca 2015; contamination reporting standards for low-biomass samples (Fierer 2025 Nat Microbiol).

     6	# genes on contigs that the representative no longer contains. Bakta renames contigs, so retained contigs are

    36	R, W, DB = sys.argv[1:4]
    37	TREE = "/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/EmptyQuarter_Data/processed/sequencing-results/sites"

    40	RIBO = re.compile(r"^((30S|50S) ribosomal protein|(Large|Small) ribosomal subunit protein) ")

    42	def parent(rep):
    43	    sample = rep.split("__")[0]; m = meta[sample]
    44	    tok, local = rep.split("__", 1)[1].split("_", 1)
    45	    anno = "single_sample" if tok == "single" else "self_supervised"
    46	    lc = local[:-4]
    47	    return f"{TREE}/{m['trip']}/site{m['site']}/{sample}/wgs/annotation/{anno}/bakta/{lc}/{lc}"

    49	def fasta(path):
    50	    name, seq = None, []
    51	    for line in open(path):
    52	        if line.startswith(">"):
    53	            if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
    54	            name, seq = line[1:].rstrip("\n"), []
    55	        else: seq.append(line.strip())
    56	    if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
    57	
    58	md5 = lambda s: hashlib.md5(s.upper().encode()).hexdigest()
    59	subs = sorted(os.path.basename(p)[:-3] for p in glob.glob(f"{REPS}/*_sub.fa"))
    60	db_ids = set()

    75	        ret = set()

    82	            if not m: continue

     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3), read-based guild abundance -- stage R3: per-sample genome-equivalent table.
     3	
     4	For each sample and identity floor (50/60/70%), reads per kilobase (RPK, subject length in nt)
     5	are summed per label; each marker is divided by the median RPK of the 14 single-copy ribosomal
     6	protein genes, giving copies per genome equivalent. For single-copy markers this approximates the
     7	fraction of genomes carrying the capacity.
     8	
     9	    python3 lifeonair/code/r3_aggregate.py lifeonair/results/reads [EXCLUDE_REPS OUT_NAME [LABELS]]
    10	
    11	LABELS (default db/marker_labels.tsv) selects the label table. With db/marker_labels_v2.tsv (corrected
    12	CoxL forms, fix_coxL_v2.py; review round 1) group 2a [NiFe] is reported in its own column hydB_2a and is
    13	no longer part of the hydB_other_nife control (R1-02). Use "-" for EXCLUDE_REPS to exclude nothing.
    14	
    15	With EXCLUDE_REPS (one rep id or "rep|protein" id per line), reads whose best hit is an EQ protein of an
    16	excluded rep, or an excluded protein, are discarded before normalisation (contamination and weak-call
    17	sensitivity); output goes to OUT_NAME.
    18	"""
    19	import csv
    20	import statistics
    21	import sys
    22	from collections import Counter
    23	from pathlib import Path
    24	
    25	HA = {"_NiFe__Group_1h": "hydB_1h", "_NiFe__Group_1l": "hydB_1l"}
    26	
    27	
    28	def main(root: Path, exclude: set, out_name: str, labels: str = "db/marker_labels.tsv") -> None:
    29	    v2 = labels.endswith("_v2.tsv")
    30	    ha = dict(HA, **({"_NiFe__Group_2a": "hydB_2a"} if v2 else {}))
    31	    lab = {r["subject"]: (r["marker"], r["label"])
    32	           for r in csv.DictReader(open(root / labels), delimiter="\t")}
    33	    nt = {r["subject"]: 3 * int(r["length_aa"])
    34	          for r in csv.DictReader(open(root / "db/subject_lengths.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    35	    cols = ["sample", "id_floor", "reads_screened", "scg_genes_detected", "scg_median_rpk",
    36	            "hydB_ha", "hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "hydB_other_nife", "coxL_formI", "coxL_other",
    37	            "reads_hydB_1l", "reads_hydB_1h", "reads_hydB_other_nife", "reads_coxL_formI",
    38	            "reads_coxL_other", "reads_scg"] + (["hydB_2a", "reads_hydB_2a"] if v2 else [])
    39	    out = open(root / out_name, "w")
    40	    out.write("\t".join(cols) + "\n")
    41	    n_done = 0
    42	    for sd in sorted((root / "per_sample").iterdir()):
    43	        if not (sd / "subject_counts.tsv").exists():
    44	            continue
    45	        meta = next(csv.DictReader(open(sd / "meta.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    46	        rows = [line.rstrip("\n").split("\t") for line in open(sd / "subject_counts.tsv")]
    47	        n_done += 1
    48	        for k, floor in ((1, 50), (2, 60), (3, 70)):
    49	            rpk, reads = Counter(), Counter()
    50	            for r in rows:
    51	                if exclude and r[0].startswith("EQ|") and (r[0].split("|")[2] in exclude or "|".join(r[0].split("|")[2:4]) in exclude):
    52	                    continue
    53	                m, label = lab[r[0]]
    54	                c = int(r[k])
    55	                if not c:
    56	                    continue
    57	                key = (m, label)
    58	                if m == "hydB":
    59	                    key = ("hydB", ha.get(label, "hydB_other_nife" if "NiFe" in label else "other"))
    60	                elif m == "coxL":
    61	                    key = ("coxL", "coxL_formI" if label == "form_I" else "coxL_other")
    62	                rpk[key] += c / (nt[r[0]] / 1000)
    63	                reads[key] += c
    64	            scg = [v for (m, _), v in rpk.items() if m == "SCG"]
    65	            med = statistics.median(scg) if len(scg) >= 10 else float("nan")
    66	            ge = lambda key: rpk[key] / med if med == med and med > 0 else float("nan")
    67	            ha_ge = ge(("hydB", "hydB_1l")) + ge(("hydB", "hydB_1h"))
    68	            out.write("\t".join(str(x) for x in [
    69	                meta["sample"], floor, meta["reads_screened"], len(scg), f"{med:.2f}",
    70	                f"{ha_ge:.5f}", f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_1l')):.5f}", f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_1h')):.5f}",
    71	                f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_other_nife')):.5f}", f"{ge(('coxL', 'coxL_formI')):.5f}",
    72	                f"{ge(('coxL', 'coxL_other')):.5f}",
    73	                reads[("hydB", "hydB_1l")], reads[("hydB", "hydB_1h")], reads[("hydB", "hydB_other_nife")],
    74	                reads[("coxL", "coxL_formI")], reads[("coxL", "coxL_other")],
    75	                sum(v for (m, _), v in reads.items() if m == "SCG")]
    76	                + ([f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_2a')):.5f}", reads[("hydB", "hydB_2a")]] if v2 else [])) + "\n")
    77	    out.close()
    78	    print(f"aggregated {n_done} samples -> {root / out_name}")
    79	
    80	
    81	if __name__ == "__main__":
    82	    excl = set(open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()) if len(sys.argv) > 2 and sys.argv[2] != "-" else set()
    83	    main(Path(sys.argv[1]), excl, sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv",
    84	         sys.argv[4] if len(sys.argv) > 4 else "db/marker_labels.tsv")
     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3), analysis 5 summary: operon features of typed hydB/coxL by class.
     3	
     4	Reports all loci and, separately, loci with >= 5 genes on both sides on the contig (complete
     5	context), because absent partners near a contig end mostly reflect assembly fragmentation.
     6	Also lists the most frequent neighbours within +/-5 genes of high-affinity hydB.
     7	
     8	    python3 lifeonair/code/operon_summary.py lifeonair/results/operon [lifeonair/results/inputs/coxL_class_v2.tsv]
     9	
    10	With the corrected CoxL calls (review round 1, R1-01) as second argument, CoxL loci are relabelled form I /
    11	form II / unresolved, group 2a is reported separately (R1-02), and the outputs get a _v2 suffix.
    12	"""
    13	import csv
    14	import gzip
    15	import sys
    16	from collections import Counter, defaultdict
    17	from pathlib import Path
    18	
    19	
    20	V2 = {}
    21	
    22	
    23	def klass(r):
    24	    c = r["class"]
    25	    if r["marker"] == "hydB":
    26	        if V2:
    27	            return ("hydB_1h" if c.endswith("Group_1h") else "hydB_1l" if c.endswith("Group_1l")
    28	                    else "hydB_2a" if c.endswith("Group_2a") else "hydB_other")
    29	        return "hydB_1h" if c.endswith("Group_1h") else "hydB_1l" if c.endswith("Group_1l") else "hydB_other"
    30	    if V2:
    31	        return {"form_I": "coxL_formI", "form_II": "coxL_formII"}.get(V2[f"{r['rep']}|{r['protein_id']}"], "coxL_unresolved")
    32	    return "coxL_formI" if c == "form_I" else "coxL_other"
    33	
    34	
    35	def pct(v, f):
    36	    return f"{100 * sum(map(f, v)) / len(v):.1f}" if v else "nan"
    37	
    38	
    39	def main(d: Path, sfx: str = "") -> None:
    40	    rows = list(csv.DictReader(open(d / "marker_context.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    41	    groups = defaultdict(list)
    42	    for r in rows:
    43	        k = klass(r)
    44	        groups[(k, "all")].append(r)
    45	        if int(r["genes_to_contig_end"]) >= 5:
    46	            groups[(k, "complete_context")].append(r)
    47	    with open(d / f"operon_summary{sfx}.tsv", "w") as o:
    48	        o.write("class\tsubset\tn\tsmall_subunit_adjacent_pct\tany_maturation_pct\tcoxM_pct\tcoxS_pct\t"
    49	                "coxMSL_pct\tcox_accessory_pct\tany_hypothetical_pct\n")
    50	        for (k, s), v in sorted(groups.items()):
    51	            o.write("\t".join([k, s, str(len(v)),
    52	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["small_subunit_adjacent"] == "1"),
    53	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_maturation"]) > 0),
    54	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["coxM"] == "1"), pct(v, lambda r: r["coxS"] == "1"),
    55	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["coxM"] == "1" and r["coxS"] == "1"),
    56	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_cox_accessory"]) > 0),
    57	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_hypothetical"]) > 0)]) + "\n")
    58	    ctx = {(r["rep"], r["protein_id"]): klass(r) for r in rows}
    59	    nb = Counter(); nloc = Counter()
    60	    for r in csv.DictReader(gzip.open(d / "neighbours.tsv.gz", "rt"), delimiter="\t"):
    61	        k = ctx[(r["rep"], r["protein_id"])]
    62	        off = int(r["offset"])
    63	        if off == 0:
    64	            nloc[k] += 1
    65	            continue
    66	        if abs(off) > 5 or k not in ("hydB_1h", "hydB_1l"):
    67	            continue
    68	        name = r["eggnog_name"] if r["eggnog_name"] not in ("-", "") else (r["bakta_gene"] or r["bakta_product"])
    69	        nb[(k, name)] += 1
    70	    with open(d / f"hydB_ha_top_neighbours{sfx}.tsv", "w") as o:
    71	        o.write("class\tneighbour\tcount\tper_locus\n")
    72	        for (k, name), c in nb.most_common():
    73	            if c >= 20:
    74	                o.write(f"{k}\t{name}\t{c}\t{c / nloc[k]:.3f}\n")
    75	    print(open(d / f"operon_summary{sfx}.tsv").read())
    76	
    77	
    78	if __name__ == "__main__":
    79	    if len(sys.argv) > 2:
    80	        V2.update({r["query"]: r["form_call"] for r in csv.DictReader(open(sys.argv[2]), delimiter="\t")})
    81	    main(Path(sys.argv[1]), "_v2" if len(sys.argv) > 2 else "")

     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3): supplementary LaTeX table bodies from the result tables (revised after review round 1).
     3	
     4	    python3 lifeonair/code/make_tables.py   (from the repository root)
     5	
     6	Writes lifeonair/manuscript/tab_benchmark.tex, tab_sensitivity.tex and tab_lineages.tex (rows only; the
     7	table environments are in main.tex). Inputs: results/benchmark/classifier_benchmark_summary.tsv,
     8	results/h123_v2/sensitivity.tsv and results/h123_v2/h1_lineages_1l_carriers.tsv.
     9	"""
    10	import csv
    11	from pathlib import Path
    12	
    13	RES = Path("lifeonair/results")
    14	OUT = Path("lifeonair/manuscript")
    15	LABEL = {"primary": "Primary (60\\% identity)", "id50": "50\\% identity", "id70": "70\\% identity",
    16	         "noflagged": "Contamination-flagged removed", "strict1l": "Weak group 1l calls removed",
    17	         "without_trip5": "Without expedition 5", "without_trip4": "Without expedition 4"}
    18	GROUP = {"hydB_1l": "{[NiFe]} group 1l", "hydB_1h": "{[NiFe]} group 1h", "hydB_2a": "{[NiFe]} group 2a",
    19	         "other_nife": "Other {[NiFe]}", "coxL_formI": "CoxL form I", "coxL_other": "Other CoxL"}
    20	
    21	
    22	def sci(p: float) -> str:
    23	    m, e = f"{p:.1e}".split("e")
    24	    return f"${m}\\times10^{{{int(e)}}}$"
    25	
    26	
    27	rd = lambda p: list(csv.DictReader(open(p), delimiter="\t"))
    28	
    29	b = {(r["scheme"], r["group"]): r for r in rd(RES / "benchmark/classifier_benchmark_summary.tsv") if r["id_floor"] == "60"}
    30	with open(OUT / "tab_benchmark.tex", "w") as o:
    31	    for g, lab in GROUP.items():
    32	        o.write(" & ".join([lab] + [f"{float(b[(s, g)][k]):.3f}" for s in ("genome", "clu50")
    33	                                    for k in ("recall", "precision")]) + "\\\\\n")
    34	
    35	with open(OUT / "tab_sensitivity.tex", "w") as o:
    36	    for r in rd(RES / "h123_v2/sensitivity.tsv"):
    37	        o.write(" & ".join([LABEL.get(r["variant"], r["variant"]), r["n_samples"],
    38	                            f"{float(r['root_1l']):.2f}", f"{float(r['root_excess_1l']):.2f}",
    39	                            sci(float(r["root_excess_1l_p"])), f"{float(r['root_excess_1h']):.2f}",
    40	                            f"{float(r['root_excess_formI']):.2f}", f"{float(r['surface_excess_1l']):.2f}",
    41	                            f"{float(r['rho_bulk_1l_ERA5']):.2f}", f"{float(r['rho_bulk_1l_POWER']):.2f}"]) + "\\\\\n")
    42	
    43	with open(OUT / "tab_lineages.tex", "w") as o:
    44	    for r in rd(RES / "h123_v2/h1_lineages_1l_carriers.tsv"):
    45	        phylum, order = r["order"].split(";")
    46	        o.write(" & ".join([phylum, order, f"{float(r['deep_mean_pct_mapped']):.2f}",
    47	                            f"{float(r['median_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep']):.2f}",
    48	                            f"{100 * float(r['frac_cells_below0']):.0f}\\%"]) + "\\\\\n")
    49	print("tables written to", OUT)

     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3): daily ERA5 precipitation 1995-01-01 to 2025-10-31 (review round 1, R1-03).
     3	
     4	The frozen climate record (amplicon cache climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet, Open-Meteo archive, ERA5
     5	0.25 deg) ends on 2024-12-31, so antecedent precipitation was missing for the October 2025 expedition. It
     6	was also fetched with Open-Meteo's default "best_match" model, which blends ERA5 with ECMWF IFS from 2017, and
     7	re-querying that default on 12 Oct 2026 returns different 2024 totals (site 1: 22.9 mm vs 102.7 mm frozen).
     8	This script fetches pure ERA5 (models=era5) for the whole period at the site coordinates, so long-term MAP
     9	and antecedent sums come from one consistent reanalysis. Only site coordinates are sent.
    10	
    11	    python3 lifeonair/code/fetch_era5_2025.py
    12	Writes lifeonair/results/inputs/era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv (site, date, P) and era5_daily_1995_2025.urls.tsv
    13	(request URL per site). Resumable: sites already in the output are skipped.
    14	"""
    15	import csv
    16	import json
    17	import time
    18	import urllib.request
    19	
    20	IN = "lifeonair/results/inputs"
    21	URL = ("https://archive-api.open-meteo.com/v1/archive?latitude={lat:.5f}&longitude={lon:.5f}"
    22	       "&start_date=1995-01-01&end_date=2025-10-31&daily=precipitation_sum&models=era5&timezone=UTC")
    23	
    24	import os
    25	sites = list(csv.DictReader(open(f"{IN}/env_site.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    26	OUT = f"{IN}/era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv"
    27	done = {l.split("\t")[0] for l in open(OUT)} if os.path.exists(OUT) else set()
    28	new = not done
    29	with open(OUT, "a") as out, open(f"{IN}/era5_daily_1995_2025.urls.tsv", "a") as uo:
    30	    if new:
    31	        out.write("site\tdate\tP\n")
    32	        uo.write("site\turl\n")
    33	    for s in sites:
    34	        if s["site"] in done:
    35	            continue
    36	        url = URL.format(lat=float(s["lat"]), lon=float(s["lon"]))
    37	        for attempt in range(5):
    38	            try:
    39	                d = json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(url, timeout=60))
    40	                break
    41	            except Exception:
    42	                time.sleep(30 * (attempt + 1))
    43	        else:
    44	            raise SystemExit(f"failed: site {s['site']}")
    45	        for day, p in zip(d["daily"]["time"], d["daily"]["precipitation_sum"]):
    46	            out.write(f"{s['site']}\t{day}\t{'' if p is None else p}\n")
    47	        uo.write(f"{s['site']}\t{url}\n")
    48	        out.flush(); uo.flush()
    49	        time.sleep(5.0)
    50	print("wrote", f"{IN}/era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv")

     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3): compact environmental inputs for H1-H3, frozen as TSV with checksums.
     3	# Sources (amplicon repository cache; sha256 recorded in lifeonair/results/inputs/env_sources.sha256):
     4	#   climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet  daily T and P per site (Open-Meteo archive default "best_match",
     5	#                                         i.e. ERA5 blended with ECMWF IFS from 2017; kept as map_bestmatch_mm)
     6	#   inputs/era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv       pure ERA5 daily P 1995-01-01..2025-10-31 (fetch_era5_2025.py; review
     7	#                                         round 1, R1-03): primary source of MAP (1995-2024) and antecedent sums
     8	#   metadata_with_rainfall.parquet        per amplicon sample: trip_date (sampling date)
     9	#   ndvi_by_sample.parquet, smap_by_sample.parquet   MODIS NDVI (60 d) and SMAP soil moisture
    10	#   srtm_topo.parquet                     site lat, lon, elevation
    11	# Metagenome sample ids equal the amplicon ids without the "eNNNN_" prefix.
    12	# Outputs: inputs/env_site.tsv (site climate normals), inputs/env_site_trip.tsv (sampling date,
    13	# antecedent precipitation, NDVI/SMAP per site x trip).
    14	#
    15	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/prep_environment.R
    16	
    17	suppressMessages(library(arrow))
    18	C   <- path.expand("~/Documents/papers/empty-quarter-amplicon/repository/cache")
    19	IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
    20	rd  <- function(f) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", stringsAsFactors = FALSE, check.names = FALSE)
    21	
    22	clim <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet")))
    23	clim$date <- as.Date(clim$date)
    24	clim$year <- as.integer(format(clim$date, "%Y"))
    25	ann <- aggregate(P ~ site + year, data = clim, FUN = sum)
    26	site <- data.frame(site = sort(unique(clim$site)))
    27	site$map_bestmatch_mm <- tapply(ann$P, ann$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
    28	e5 <- rd(file.path(IN, "era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv")); e5$date <- as.Date(e5$date)
    29	e5$year <- as.integer(format(e5$date, "%Y"))
    30	ann5 <- aggregate(P ~ site + year, data = e5[e5$year <= 2024, ], FUN = sum)
    31	site$map_era5_mm  <- tapply(ann5$P, ann5$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
    32	site$mat_era5_c   <- tapply(clim$T, clim$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
    33	site$rainy_days_per_yr <- tapply(clim$P >= 1, clim$site, sum)[as.character(site$site)] / length(unique(clim$year))
    34	topo <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "srtm_topo.parquet")))
    35	site <- merge(site, topo, by = "site", all.x = TRUE)
    36	
    37	md <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet")))
    38	md$sample_id <- sub("^e[0-9]+_", "", md$sample)
    39	md$trip_date <- as.Date(md$trip_date)
    40	nd <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "ndvi_by_sample.parquet")))
    41	sm <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "smap_by_sample.parquet")))
    42	md <- merge(md, nd, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE); md <- merge(md, sm, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE)
    43	
    44	# antecedent precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end); pure ERA5 is
    45	# primary, the best_match record (ends 2024) is kept for sensitivity
    46	ante <- function(s, d, days, cl = e5) {
    47	  x <- cl[cl$site == s & cl$date < d & cl$date >= d - days, ]
    48	  if (d - 1 > max(cl$date)) return(NA_real_)
    49	  sum(x$P)
    50	}
    51	# per site x trip: sampling date (earliest amplicon date in that cell) and site-level remote sensing
    52	st <- aggregate(trip_date ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = min)
    53	st$trip_date <- as.Date(st$trip_date, origin = "1970-01-01")
    54	rs <- aggregate(cbind(ndvi_W60d, evi_W60d, smap_sm_W30d) ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = median, na.action = na.pass)
    55	st <- merge(st, rs, by = c("site", "trip"), all.x = TRUE)
    56	# antecedent ERA5 precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end)
    57	st$P90_era5  <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 90)
    58	st$P365_era5 <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 365)
    59	st$P365_bestmatch <- mapply(function(a, b) ante(a, b, 365, clim), st$site, st$trip_date)
    60	st$trip <- paste0("trip", st$trip)
    61	
    62	write.table(site, file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    63	write.table(st, file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    64	src <- c(file.path(IN, "era5_daily_1995_2025.tsv"), file.path(C, c("climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet", "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet",
    65	                      "ndvi_by_sample.parquet", "smap_by_sample.parquet", "srtm_topo.parquet")))
    66	writeLines(vapply(src, function(f) system2("sha256sum", f, stdout = TRUE), ""), file.path(IN, "env_sources.sha256"))
    67	mg <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
    68	cov <- sum(paste(mg$site, mg$trip) %in% paste(st$site, st$trip))
    69	cat(sprintf("sites %d; site x trip cells %d (P365 available %d); metagenome samples covered %d of %d\n",
    70	            nrow(site), nrow(st), sum(!is.na(st$P365_era5)), cov, nrow(mg)))
     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3): orders of group 1l carriers beside roots (Table S3), revised after review round 1
     3	# (sites 1-60, carriers restricted to retained contigs). Share of the mapped community held by the group 1l
     4	# carriers of each order per sample (CoverM), median over replicates per site visit, log2 ratio of
     5	# root-adjacent to bulk (pseudocount 0.001 percentage points), for the ten orders with the highest bulk share.
     6	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/lineages_v2.R   -> lifeonair/results/h123_v2/h1_lineages_1l_carriers.tsv
     7	IN <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
     8	rd <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, quote = "", comment.char = "", check.names = FALSE, ...)
     9	ab <- rd(gzfile(file.path(IN, "coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz")), header = FALSE, col.names = c("sample", "rep", "abund"))
    10	ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund), ]
    11	tot <- tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
    12	cls <- rd(file.path(IN, "atmo_classification_v2.tsv"))
    13	r1l <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1l$", cls$assignment)])
    14	tax <- rbind(rd(file.path(IN, "r232_bac120.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2], rd(file.path(IN, "r232_ar53.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2])
    15	rk <- function(cl, p) ifelse(grepl(paste0(p, "__[^;]"), cl), sub(paste0(".*", p, "__([^;]*).*"), "\\1", cl), "unassigned")
    16	ab <- ab[ab$rep %in% r1l, ]
    17	cl <- tax$classification[match(ab$rep, tax$user_genome)]
    18	ab$order <- paste(rk(cl, "p"), rk(cl, "o"), sep = ";")
    19	meta <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv")); meta <- meta[!is.na(meta$site) & meta$site %in% 1:60, ]
    20	ge <- rd("lifeonair/results/reads/guild_genome_equivalents_v2.tsv"); ge <- ge[ge$id_floor == 60 & ge$scg_genes_detected >= 10, ]
    21	meta <- meta[meta$sample_id %in% ge$sample & meta$sample_id %in% names(tot), ]
    22	sh <- tapply(ab$abund, list(ab$sample, ab$order), sum); sh[is.na(sh)] <- 0
    23	sh <- 100 * sh[match(meta$sample_id, rownames(sh)), , drop = FALSE] / as.numeric(tot[meta$sample_id]); sh[is.na(sh)] <- 0
    24	deep <- meta$compartment == "Deep"
    25	top <- names(sort(colMeans(sh[deep, ]), decreasing = TRUE))[1:10]
    26	vis <- paste(meta$site, meta$trip)
    27	out <- do.call(rbind, lapply(top, function(o) {
    28	  md <- tapply(sh[, o], list(vis, meta$compartment), median)
    29	  ok <- is.finite(md[, "Deep"]) & is.finite(md[, "PlantRoot"])
    30	  l <- log2((md[ok, "PlantRoot"] + 0.001) / (md[ok, "Deep"] + 0.001))
    31	  data.frame(order = o, deep_mean_pct_mapped = mean(sh[deep, o]), n_visits = sum(ok),
    32	             median_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep = median(l), frac_cells_below0 = mean(l < 0))
    33	}))
    34	write.table(out, "lifeonair/results/h123_v2/h1_lineages_1l_carriers.tsv", sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    35	print(out)

**Part 1 — Review**

The manuscript titled “Plant roots and long-term aridity set the atmospheric hydrogen-scavenging capacity of hyperarid Rub’ al-Khali soils” combines a 5,438-genome catalogue with read-based functional profiling of 550 metagenomes. It reports a pronounced root-associated reduction in group-1l hydrogenase abundance, contrasting distributions of hydrogen and carbon monoxide oxidation genes, and an association between group-1l abundance and long-term precipitation. Vegetation indices and predicted growth traits are used to investigate possible mechanisms. The strongest contribution is the replicated, depth-matched group-1l contrast. However, an inverted CoxL classification rule compromises the CO results, and the temporal analysis covers substantially less time than the manuscript implies.

I recommend **major revision**. The root-associated group-1l pattern appears recoverable, but the manuscript’s present three-hypothesis narrative is not supported. H2 requires reconstruction after correcting CoxL classification. H3 must distinguish an association with spatial precipitation from evidence about temporal responses or climate-change substitution. Several mechanistic statements exceed what these observational, relative-abundance data establish.

The audit reproduced the original read aggregation to its reported precision: maximum GE discrepancy was below \(5\times10^{-6}\). All 573 archived screening metadata records specified 100-nt truncation and a 25-aa minimum alignment; the archive and local database tables matched their recorded checksums. I also reproduced the principal group-1l contrast and spatial correlation. These checks establish numerical traceability, not biological validity of the labels. I did not rerun DIAMOND, tree inference, or the complete R workflows. No files were modified. The PDF integrity scan detected no reviewer-directed instructions; its manuscript flags concerned small figure text.

Major issues follow.

**Marker identity must be corrected before interpreting the ecology (R1-01, R1-02).** The CoxL classifier reverses the diagnostic motifs. `AYRGAGR` is associated with form II, whereas the characteristic form-I motif is `AYXCSFR`. This distinction is explicit in primary sequence analyses. [Martin-Cuadrado et al.](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2786428/) The error is consequential: 1,627 of 1,901 reported form-I proteins were admitted solely by `AYRGAGR`, without passing the identity/coverage criterion.

I performed a diagnostic relabelling in memory, retaining the identity/coverage criterion and recognising the form-I motif, without changing alignments. This reduced the candidate form-I set to 285 proteins. Approximately 90.1% of reads originally assigned to form I across the screened libraries were reassigned. Bulk-soil median form-I GE changed from 0.616 to 0.105. The surface/bulk contrast changed from −0.020 to −0.502 log₂ units, with site-level Wilcoxon \(p=0.00106\). The root/bulk contrast became −0.344, \(p=0.0284\). These are diagnostic recalculations, not a substitute for curated reclassification, but they invalidate the current claim of position-independent CO capacity. The group-1l root contrast remained approximately −2.23, and its excess remained approximately −1.97 under this correction.

The hydrogenase control also needs revision. Group 2a includes experimentally demonstrated atmospheric H₂ oxidisers, yet all group-2a reads enter the supposedly non-high-affinity control. [Islam et al.](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7784904/) The dataset contains 140 group-2a proteins, and their reads account for 24.1% of the primary cohort’s other-[NiFe] control reads. Moreover, Grinter et al., cited for the introductory enzyme statement, characterise Huc, a group-2a enzyme. Restricting the focal comparison to groups 1h and 1l is legitimate; defining them as the complete atmospheric-H₂ guild and placing group 2a in a negative control is not.

**The temporal test is not a five-expedition, 31-month test (R1-03, R1-04, R1-11).** All expedition-5 antecedent precipitation values are missing because the climate record ends in 2024. The bulk-soil temporal analysis contains 71 visits at 27 sites between March 2023 and August 2024, approximately 17 months. Fourteen sites contribute only two visits. This exclusion must be explicit in the abstract, methods, results, and interpretation, or the precipitation record must be extended.

Comparing a 30-year spatial climate normal with preceding-year rainfall does not directly test the response to a sustained change in aridity. Nor does a nonsignificant temporal coefficient establish independence from rain. The manuscript also compares the temporal confidence interval with a spatial point estimate rather than estimating their difference. My joint site bootstrap retained a positive temporal-minus-spatial difference, approximately 1.05–6.31, so propagating spatial uncertainty alone does not eliminate that contrast. However, this still compares different environmental quantities and treats sites as independent despite only four shared expeditions. The defensible conclusion is a difference between the sampled spatial and short-term associations, subject to the design’s limitations.

Within-site centring does not make visit residuals independent. Their ordinary Spearman \(p\)-values remain inappropriate for repeated observations. Conversely, the trip-adjusted sample-level mixed model uses uncentred precipitation, mixing within-site and between-site information. These analyses should be replaced by an explicit within/between decomposition with the relevant replication structure.

**The hypotheses are exploratory, and the primary cohort is inconsistently defined (R1-05, R1-06).** The results README states that the original hypotheses were contradicted and replacement hypotheses were developed after reassessing the same data. Repeated statements that results occurred “as H1/H3 predicted” therefore misrepresent the inferential sequence. Report this as exploratory hypothesis development and distinguish any genuinely independent validation.

The 61-location analysis includes four samples from sites 62 and 64. The companion data descriptor identifies these as special-interest locations outside the repeated 1–60 transect; the site-alias table identifies a road location and a campground. Restricting to sites 1–60 gives 546 samples at 59 sites and 106 root/bulk visits at 47 sites. The group-1l contrast remains strong, approximately −2.275 log₂, but the manuscript needs a consistent sampling frame and a documented inclusion ledger.

**Relative depletion does not establish loss of organisms or selection against the enzyme (R1-07, R1-08, R1-09).** GE is a relative marker-copy measure, and the lineage analyses are also compositional. The available DNA records make biomass differences a concrete concern: after excluding conflicting/nonpositive concentration records, 48 paired site visits had a median root/bulk DNA-concentration ratio of approximately 9.78. This is not a valid absolute microbial-biomass correction, because extraction and plant DNA can contribute, but it demonstrates why lower copies per genome cannot be equated with fewer carriers per gram.

Matching depth removes one confounder. It does not isolate plant carbon from moisture, oxygen, soil properties, plant identity, or microsite selection. The statements that plants “select against” group 1l and that aridity “sets” its abundance require stronger evidence or associative wording. Likewise, attenuation after adjustment for composition PC1 does not demonstrate mediation through lineage composition. It is compatible with mediation, confounding, and adjustment for a variable partly constructed from the same carrier abundances.

**The mechanism analyses do not quantify the contribution of plant carbon (R1-10, R1-22).** A 250-m NDVI measurement and predicted maximal growth potential are imperfect proxies for carbon availability at a root microsite. A residual intercept of −1.59 does not estimate the fraction of the effect unexplained by carbon, particularly without measurement-error treatment or a mediation model. “Plant-carbon proxies explain a minor part” should be replaced by a narrower statement about the observed associations.

The genus analysis also needs stronger treatment of phylogeny, prevalence, and uncertainty. It treats 288 genus summaries as observations, but 123 have exactly zero median fold change and 31 genera have only one representative contributing to their traits. The response, growth predictor, and carriage predictors all depend on the recovered MAG catalogue, with incomplete genomes treated as non-carriers when a marker is absent. Phylum adjustment cannot establish an enzyme-specific effect beyond lineage ecology.

**Several statistical analyses do not respect the experimental structure (R1-12–R1-18).** The main paired H1 analysis appropriately resamples sites. This protection is not carried consistently into the NDVI, H2, H3, and mechanism analyses, where co-located samples share environmental values but several models omit a site-visit effect. The spatial bootstrap over six blocks is useful sensitivity evidence, but needs residual spatial diagnostics and sensitivity to block definition. Independent climate products support the spatial association; they do not validate the temporal rainfall exposure.

The PERMANOVA uses unrestricted permutations, although samples are nested within visits and repeatedly collected at sites. Its 29.4% site fraction and 2.0% expedition fraction also correspond to very different numbers of factor degrees of freedom. Their ratio is not evidence that lineages persisted or that ecological integration takes longer than 31 months.

The synchrony calculation pools positions within each visit despite strong position effects and uneven sampling. Changes in compartment representation can therefore generate synchrony. More generally, synchrony measures covariation, not persistence.

Even before correcting CoxL, its compartment LRT was significant, \(p=4.87\times10^{-6}\). A changing H₂/CO ratio demonstrates different proportional responses, not independent regulation or equivalence of CO abundance across positions. Finally, secondary tests require multiplicity control. For example, the highlighted surface-CoxL temporal \(p=0.00983\) becomes approximately 0.0738 under BH adjustment across just the nine trip-adjusted marker-by-position tests.

**Sequence provenance and measurement validity need further work (R1-19–R1-21, R1-23, R1-24).** The `_sub` annotation resolvers can retrieve parent-bin annotations without restricting them to retained contigs. Two representatives explicitly removed from the official hydrogenase guild still contribute three group-1l proteins to the read database. The growth workflow also processes 1,010 `_sub` representatives through this resolver. The catalogue census, trait analyses, and sequence database therefore need reconciliation at the retained-contig level.

Competitive matching within this targeted database does not establish sensitivity and specificity against broader homologous families. The marker and ribosomal reference sets also have different coverage and clustering procedures. Benchmark the short-read classifier and GE calibration, particularly across the taxonomic compositions being compared.

The assertion that the shared denominator cancels exactly is false for the implemented pseudocount-adjusted ratios of replicate medians. Without a pseudocount, the paired group-1l median is −2.529 rather than −2.231; no paired cell requires a pseudocount for that contrast. The qualitative pattern survives, but the headline magnitude and invariance argument require qualification.

Contamination screening is incomplete across batches, and the nine available PMA pairs are not analysed. These limitations matter particularly for claims about persistence and root-associated loss in low-biomass soil.

**The literature supports a narrower novelty claim and a less directional moisture mechanism (R1-25, R1-26).** Li et al. already combine desert shrub contrasts, carbon measurements/additions, trace-gas uptake, and life-history traits. López et al. directly establish predicted growth potential as a major correlate of rhizosphere enrichment. The contribution here should centre on the replicated group-resolved association in this transect, after classification correction. Li et al. also performed hydrogenase subgroup typing; their reported groups require explicit comparison rather than characterising their analysis simply as one undifferentiated hydrogen function.

Jordaan et al. report relative declines in group-1l genes/transcripts after hydration, but also strongly stimulated H₂ uptake and explicitly caution about relative expression. Imminger et al. report increased group-1l transcription during hydration. These studies do not support a general rule that wetter root-adjacent soil suppresses group-1l physiology. Stable DNA composition can coexist with rapid metabolic responses.

**Reproducibility remains incomplete despite the useful frozen outputs (R1-27, R1-28).** The archived counts and checksums are valuable. However, the advertised fetch route requires KAUST credentials, upstream typing is outside the supplied analysis-code directory, several inputs use private absolute paths, and P3 claims are absent from the repository’s claim-verification harness. The sensitivity code also does not rerun all H1–H3 analyses as the methods claim: it omits the temporal analyses and the primary H2 raw-read-ratio contrast.

Minor issues follow.

The operon screen establishes neighbourhood co-occurrence, not complete operons: it does not require the reported gene order or strand consistency (R1-29). Figures need larger text at final manuscript size, annotated phylogenetic support/reference groups, uncertainty intervals, and a temporal panel corresponding to the headline H3 claim (R1-30). The reported compartment “variance shares” omit covariance between the compartment and expedition fitted components, so they should not be presented as a standard partition of explained variance (R1-31).

The README and manuscript should agree on the current surface contrast and spatial bootstrap interval; distinguish median-effect intervals from tests on site means (R1-32). Finally, Jordaan’s study is Australian, not Gobi, and predicted maximal growth potential should consistently be distinguished from observed growth in these soils (R1-33).

The site-count discrepancy is a join bug: `topography.tsv` contains two rows for site 19, and the spatial analysis duplicates that site when merging. Recomputing with unique sites changes the bulk group-1l correlation from −0.373 to −0.401. I also confirmed that the benchmark retains external references during cluster holdout, so it does not implement the manuscript’s claim that all proteins sharing 50% identity were removed.

def read_faa(path):
    name, seq = None, []
    with open(path) as fh:
        for line in fh:
            if line.startswith(">"):
                if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
                name = line[1:].split()[0]; seq = []
            else: seq.append(line.strip())
    if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)

out = open(f"{OUTD}/scg_raw.faa", "w")
n_rep = n_prot = miss = 0
for rep in reps:
    ap, fp = resolve(rep, "eggnog"), resolve(rep, "bakta")
    if not ap or not fp:
        miss += 1; continue
    hits = scg_proteins(ap)
    if not hits:
        continue
    n_rep += 1
    for pid, s in read_faa(fp):
        g = hits.get(pid)
        if g and len(s) >= 50:
            out.write(f">SCG|{g}|{rep}|{pid}\n{s}\n"); n_prot += 1
out.close()
print(f"[scg] reps={len(reps)} unresolved={miss} reps_with_scg={n_rep} proteins={n_prot}", flush=True)

# 2. gRodon per rep, parallel
cat > "$OUT/run_grodon.R" <<'RS'
suppressMessages({library(gRodon); library(Biostrings); library(parallel)})
args <- commandArgs(TRUE); files <- list.files(args[1], "\\.ffn$", full.names = TRUE)
one <- function(f) {
  s <- readDNAStringSet(f); s <- s[width(s) %% 3 == 0 & width(s) >= 240]
  heg <- grepl(" HEG$", names(s))
  rep <- sub("\\.ffn$", "", basename(f))
  if (sum(heg) < 10) return(data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = NA, lower = NA, upper = NA, cub_hegs = NA))
  g <- tryCatch(predictGrowth(s, heg, mode = "partial"), error = function(e) NULL)
  if (is.null(g)) return(data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = NA, lower = NA, upper = NA, cub_hegs = NA))
  data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = g$d, lower = g$LowerCI, upper = g$UpperCI, cub_hegs = g$CUBHE)
}
res <- do.call(rbind, mclapply(files, one, mc.cores = as.integer(args[3])))
write.table(res, args[2], sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
cat("[grodon] reps", nrow(res), "with estimate", sum(!is.na(res$d_hours)), "\n")
RS

kropp_2026_atmospheric-hydrogen-consumption-regulated.fulltext.xml ABSTRACT Consumption of atmospheric hydrogen (H 2 ) enables diverse aerobic microorganisms to grow and persist in resource-deprived environments. In the aerobic saprophyte  Mycobacterium smegmatis , hydrogen oxidation is catalyzed by two differentially expressed, high-affinity, oxygen-insensitive uptake hydrogenases, Huc and Hhy. Huc enables mixotrophic growth and facilitates the transition from growth to dormancy. Although the  huc  operon is known to be upregulated in response to organic carbon deprivation, the specific signals and regulators modulating its expression remain unresolved. Here, we show that GylR, a glycerol-3-phosphate-sensing regulator of glycerol metabolism, plays a role in the repression of  huc  expression in response to the availability of glycerol but not other carbon sources. Based on proteomic analyses and activity assays, mutation or knockdown of  gylR  leads to enhanced Huc production and activity. GylR and other key catabolite repressor proteins (Crp1 and Crp2) do not directly bind to the  huc  operon, indicating that repression is mediated by unidentified transcription factors, with GylR acting as an upstream sensor. Here, we present data that suggest atmospheric H 2  oxidation is regulated in response to organic carbon source availability through the process of catabolite repression. By identifying a key signal that prompts atmospheric H 2  oxidation, these findings advance understanding of how aerobic bacteria adapt to changing environmental conditions and suggest that organic carbon levels are a key factor regulating the main sink of atmospheric H 2  in soils globally. IMPORTANCE Soil microorganisms collectively consume 70 million tonnes of atmospheric hydrogen (H 2 ) a year, regulating atmospheric composition and climate change. In turn, consuming this dependable trace gas enables these microorganisms to survive even when their preferred organic energy sources are exhausted. Despite the importance of H 2  consumption for soil biodiversity and atmospheric regulation, the signals and sensors that regulate this process remain to be understood. Here, we demonstrate that a model soil bacterium turns on the machinery required for atmospheric H 2  consumption in direct response to being limited by organic carbon availability, through the process of catabolite repression. Specifically, in the absence of a sensor of the organic carbon source glycerol, a H 2 -consuming hydrogenase is highly expressed and active. These findings suggest that organic carbon levels have a major role in regulating trace gas oxidation, with implications for predicting how trace gas consumption and soil biodiversity respond to environmental change.
baril_2023_carbon-amendments-microcosms-induce.fulltext.xml Abstract High-affinity H 2 -oxidizing bacteria (HA-HOB) thriving in soil are responsible for the most important sink of atmospheric H 2 . Their activity increases with soil organic carbon content, but the incidence of different carbohydrate fractions on the process has received little attention. Here we tested the hypothesis that carbon amendments impact HA-HOB activity and diversity differentially depending on their recalcitrance and their concentration. Carbon sources (sucrose, starch, cellulose) and application doses (0, 0.1, 1, 3, 5% C eq  soil dw −1 ) were manipulated in soil microcosms. Only 0.1% C eq  soil dw −1  cellulose treatment stimulated the HA-HOB activity. Sucrose amendments induced the most significant changes, with an abatement of 50% activity at 1% C eq  soil dw −1 . This was accompanied with a loss of bacterial and fungal alpha diversity and a reduction of high-affinity group 1 h/5 [NiFe]-hydrogenase gene ( hhyL ) abundance. A quantitative classification framework was elaborated to assign carbon preference traits to 16S rRNA gene, ITS and  hhyL  genotypes. The response was uneven at the taxonomic level, making carbon preference a difficult trait to predict. Overall, the results suggest that HA-HOB activity is more susceptible to be stimulated by low doses of recalcitrant carbon, while labile carbon-rich environment is an unfavorable niche for HA-HOB, inducing catabolic repression of hydrogenase.
medinacaro_2026_metabolic-transitions-along-moisture.fulltext.xml Background The Barrancas Blancas (BB) plain, situated in the high-altitude Atacama region, is a cryogenic, hyper-arid, poly-extreme environment. The seasonal formation of a temporary freshwater lake under these harsh conditions provides a unique opportunity to examine how liquid water influences the metabolic responses of desert microbial communities. Using a metatranscriptomic approach, we characterised microbial community responses along a 70-meter natural moisture gradient. Results Along the gradient, the most active sites (8–23 m from the lake) exhibited high RNA recovery and diverse metabolic functions. Bacillariophyta (diatoms) drove oxygenic phototrophy, while Pseudomonadota contributed to anoxygenic phototrophy and nitrogen fixation. Additionally, Pseudomonadota, Actinomycetota, and Bacteroidota expressed genes for the oxidation of nitrate, sulfide, thiosulfate, and trace gases (H₂, CO). The energy derived from these processes was reflected in the high capacity for carbon fixation by these taxa. Moreover, network analysis revealed that these primary producers co-occurred with a diverse range of heterotrophic prokaryotic and eukaryotic groups. In contrast, at the driest site, hydrogen oxidation was the primary energy-conserving process, predominantly associated with Actinomycetota, which also contributed to hydrogenotrophic carbon fixation. Notably, even at this site, heterotrophic eukaryotes co-occurred with these chemolithotrophic primary producers. Conclusions This study presents the first transcriptomic analysis from the high-altitude Atacama Desert, facilitated by the favourable moisture conditions. Furthermore, these findings highlight a moisture-driven transition in microbial energy acquisition strategies and emphasise the ecological significance of both photoautotrophy and chemolithotrophy, which likely vary depending on the dynamics of temporary lakes. The BB plain and its lake thus offer a robust model for understanding microbial resilience, functional plasticity, community assembly, and trophic interactions in extreme environments, providing novel insights into life at the edge of habitability. firrincieli_2026_genomic-evidence-aerotrophy-defining.fulltext.xml Abstract Members of the class Ktedonobacteria (phylum Chloroflexota) are widespread across various terrestrial environments, including oligotrophic caves, although the genomic basis underlying this distribution remains unclear. Here, we present a systematic genomic analysis of Ktedonobacteria across ecosystems, with a focus on oligotrophic caves. Cave Ktedonobacteria belong to novel genera within the  Ktedonobacteraceae  and harbour genes associated with a mixotrophic metabolism combining the use of organic and inorganic substrates as energy and carbon sources. Comparative analyses of all available Ktedonobacteria genomes from diverse environments showed that most metabolic traits, including those associated with atmospheric gas oxidation, are primarily conserved among members of the same family. In contrast, genes involved in CO₂ fixation are enriched in Ktedonobacteria inhabiting caves. Phylogenetic analysis indicated that the RuBisCO of  Ktedonobacteria  likely represents a novel Form I subtype (named group IG) encompassing thermophilic and acidophilic bacteria from six different phyla that often inhabit similar extreme environments. The presence of this subtype across distinct lineages in comparable habitats suggests that it may confer a selective advantage in nutrient-poor settings (like caves) and that its distribution may be influenced by horizontal gene transfer. This inferred autotrophic capacity is associated with a transaldolase variant of the Calvin–Benson–Bassham cycle that was previously described only in a single Firmicutes species. Overall, this study provides genetic evidence for the potential coupling of atmospheric gas oxidation with dark CO₂ fixation in  Ktedonobacteria , highlighting their possible role in sustaining primary production in oligotrophic ecosystems, including caves.

# A2 (b) aridity: across bulk sites, genus-weight term vs within-genus term for the 1l-carrier share
bulk <- gv[gv$compartment == "Deep", ]; bulk$site <- as.integer(sub("\\|.*", "", bulk$visit))
bsg <- aggregate(cbind(w, w1) ~ site + genus, data = bulk, FUN = mean)
qbar <- aggregate(cbind(w, w1) ~ genus, data = bsg, FUN = mean); qbar$q <- ifelse(qbar$w > 0, qbar$w1 / qbar$w, 0)
bsg$qbar <- qbar$q[match(bsg$genus, qbar$genus)]
site_dec <- aggregate(cbind(obs = w1, turnover_only = w * qbar) ~ site, data = bsg, FUN = sum)
site_dec <- merge(site_dec, site_env[, c("site", "map_era5_mm")], by = "site")
lifeonair/manuscript/main.tex:187:extraction blanks of the fifth expedition were flagged, and a re-implementation of the frequency method of
# P3 "Life on air" (ISME Journal, manuscript draft)

Atmospheric H2 and CO scavenging capacity in Empty Quarter soils. Split from the MAG/dark-proteome
paper on 10 October 2026 (`../plans/manuscript-split-2026-10.md`). The repository rules in
`../CLAUDE.md` apply here too: capacity is not activity or flux, and heavy compute runs on IBEX.

Start with `results/README.md` (top section: current state after review round 1), then `reviews/` for the
critical-review loop (Astra reviews and point-by-point responses per round).

| Folder | Content |
|---|---|
| `code/` | Analysis scripts (IBEX Slurm jobs, R and Python); R environment `code/environment.yml`. `make_lifeonair_figure.py` is Alejandra's earlier catalogue-census figure. |
| `results/` | Compact inputs and outputs; per-sample read counts are fetched by `../reproducibility/fetch_large_files.sh` |
| `figures/` | Figure 1 (`fig1_guilds`), Figure 2 (`fig2_scaling`), Figs S1–S2, all from `code/fig_v2.R`; older `fig_h123`/`fig_mechanism` are superseded |
| `literature/` | Novelty-check literature (further PDFs in `../resources/literature/trace-gas-deserts/`) |
| `manuscript/` | The ISME J manuscript `main.tex` with `references.bib` and generated supplementary table rows (`make_tables.py`); text removed from P4 on 10 Oct 2026 |
| `reviews/` | Review loop: `round*/` Astra review, findings table, response letter |
| `plans/` | Earlier atmospheric-metabolism plan |

Scripts are run from the repository root, for example
`micromamba run -n eq-lifeonair Rscript lifeonair/code/h123_v2.R`.

Not moved, because other analyses and the reproducibility harness still read them:
- `../results/atmospheric/`: historical 15,469-MAG typing behind the A1 claims in `../reproducibility/claims.tsv`.
- `../RQ/D3/`: 16S-weighted capacity index.
- `../figures/fig_lifeonair_by_phylum.pdf`: made by `../code/make_figures.py`; no longer used in the P4 manuscript.
- `../notes/dossiers/06_tracegas_bgc_resistome.md`: shared with the P4 biosynthetic and resistome census.

codex
**Part 1 — Review**

The manuscript titled “Group 1l hydrogen scavengers are relatively depleted beside plant roots across the hyperarid Rub’ al-Khali” analyses 546 metagenomes from 59 sites using a 5,438-genome catalogue and read-based marker quantification. Its principal result is a root/bulk group-1l contrast of −2.275 log₂ across 106 visits at 47 sites, with an excess over reference markers of −1.827. New analyses relate this pattern to DNA concentration, genus composition and predicted growth potential, and examine surface and precipitation associations. The replicated, group-resolved relative root contrast remains the strongest contribution. The revision substantially improves the biological framing, but several new analyses contain implementation errors or support narrower conclusions than those presented.

I recommend **major revision**. The central relative-abundance observation survives independent recalculation. However, duplicated spatial records, incorrect handling of absent genera, overstated benchmark separation, incomplete contamination sensitivity and unsupported precipitation-robustness claims require correction. These are consequential problems in the revised evidence, not reasons to reopen the PI’s decisions about relative depletion, the narrowed root/surface comparison or exploratory framing.

I independently reaggregated all 1,716 usable sample-by-threshold records from the archived read counts: the maximum GE discrepancy was \(4.99994\times10^{-6}\), consistent with output rounding. All 573 screening metadata records specified 100-nt truncation and a 25-aa minimum alignment. ERA5 climatologies and antecedent sums also reproduced to numerical precision. Benchmark, genome-size and corrected-growth outputs matched their IBEX copies; the recorded jobs were 53613713, 53611804 and 53614016. I did not rerun DIAMOND, phylogenetic inference or the complete R workflows. The micromamba wrapper attempted temporary-file creation and was blocked by read-only permissions; subsequent recalculations used in-memory Python. No files were modified.

The PDF integrity scans detected no reviewer-directed instructions. Flags concerned small scientific labels, publisher marks such as `1234567890();,:`, and production headers. Figure typography remains a substantive presentation issue.

**Round-1 dispositions**

“Resolved” includes an accepted withdrawal or appropriately narrowed claim; it does not imply that the underlying biological uncertainty has disappeared.

**Major issues**

**The spatial analysis contains duplicated observations, and its headline robustness claim contradicts its own sensitivity results (R2-01–02).** `topography.tsv` contains two records for site 19. The unrestricted merge in `h123_v2.R:345` duplicates that site throughout the later spatial calculations and duplicates its nine samples in the all-sample climate model. There are actually 51 bulk, 45 surface and 59 root sites, rather than the reported 52, 46 and 60. Using unique sites, I obtain bulk group-1l ERA5 \(\rho=-0.401\), versus the saved −0.373.

More seriously, “consistently across precipitation products and spatial sensitivity analyses” is false. In the saved surface group-1l results, NASA POWER gives \(\rho=-0.317\), \(q=0.0546\), and a six-block interval of −0.67 to +0.12. The blended product gives \(\rho=-0.207\), \(q=0.253\), with every block interval crossing zero. All four surface groups have nonsignificant blended-product correlations. The abstract and Discussion must distinguish the ERA5 result from cross-product robustness. Surface group-1l association also disappears after the implemented composition-axis adjustment; that adjustment uses a bulk-derived axis and should itself be labelled explicitly.

**The benchmark does not perform the exclusion described in the manuscript (R2-03).** Only EQ marker proteins enter clustering and fold assignment. HydDB and Cordero references remain in every database. Yet every retained group-1h protein, every retained group-2a protein and 997/1,111 retained group-1l proteins have a recorded HydDB hit at ≥50% identity. Thus the claim that “all proteins sharing 50% identity with the source” were removed is demonstrably incorrect. Clustering EQ sequences also does not itself establish an exhaustive identity boundary between folds. Report the existing experiment as EQ-cluster holdout with external references retained, and perform a genuinely sequence-disjoint benchmark if claiming performance on that basis. Distinguish reproducing computational labels from independently validating enzyme function.

**The benchmark exposes substantial CoxL error, while GE calibration remains untested (R2-04–05).** Under cluster holdout at the primary threshold, there are 14,594 true-form-I fragments assigned to form I, but also 4,285 other-CoxL fragments and 24 non-marker fragments assigned there. Precision is 0.772. This is not a negligible qualification to “the read classifier was specific for these groups.” Because other CoxL is much more abundant than form I, community-dependent cross-assignment could affect comparatively small CoxL contrasts. The required check is whether those contrasts survive a more discriminating classifier or an empirically bounded error model.

Separately, marker-fragment classification does not demonstrate that summed length-normalised hits divided by SCG signal recover copies per genome across these communities. SCGs are not held out or quantitatively benchmarked. Mock communities with known marker dosage and differing taxonomic composition should test the complete estimator. Until then, GE should be described as an estimated marker-to-SCG measure rather than an exactly calibrated genome count.

**Contamination sensitivity remains incomplete despite available controls and new results (R2-08–09).** The response’s claim that blanks exist only for expedition 5 is contradicted by the companion methods, which document six expedition-4 extraction blanks linked to 97 biological profiles. The current catalogue manuscript also documents a shotgun negative control. These controls should be assessed according to their assay and batch coverage.

Furthermore, none of the five representatives flagged by `decontam_batch.R` occurs in `reads/excl_v2_noflagged.txt`. The published contamination sensitivity therefore does not test the newly reported screen. Update the exclusion union and propagate it into the relevant read-based and MAG-based analyses, reporting the consequences separately from the broad genus-based flagging.

**The genus result remains an association requiring better uncertainty treatment, and community growth estimates can represent very little of a sample (R2-10–11).** The revised genus table still contains 121 exactly zero responses among 289 genera and 32 genera represented by one genome. Removing genera because their response equals zero is outcome-based selection, not a prevalence analysis. The site bootstrap added to the growth-shift intercept does not propagate uncertainty into the genus regression.

I reproduced the reported group-1l genus coefficient, −0.812, SE 0.180. An additional weighted regression with order fixed effects retained the association, approximately −1.035, SE 0.221. This diagnostic supports the association and argues against dismissing it solely as phylum confounding. It does not resolve catalogue ascertainment, incomplete-genome non-detection or dependence among genus responses estimated from the same sites.

Growth-prediction coverage is a separate problem. Although median coverage is approximately 97.7%, 26 primary samples have coverage below 80%; sample `55Sr3` has only 1.45%. Renormalising the predicted subset gives these samples an apparently complete community growth estimate. Report coverage by position and visit, repeat the growth-shift analysis after defensible coverage restrictions, and propagate uncertainty or bound the contribution of unpredicted genomes.

**The new aridity decomposition mishandles absent genera (R2-12).** `bsg <- aggregate(..., FUN=mean)` averages each genus only over visits represented in the sparse table. Missing genus/visit combinations are not zeros. Consequently, site-level genus weights sum to a mean of 1.209 and a maximum of 2.339. These are not community shares. Zero-filling before averaging restores sums of one and changes the observed and turnover-only correlations from −0.380/−0.367 to −0.430/−0.433. The qualitative interpretation may survive, but the decomposition and downstream mixed-genus analysis require regeneration from valid compositions.

**The temporal analysis is improved, but positive rainfall associations need stronger exposure and uncertainty checks (R2-15–17).** Extending ERA5 solves the missing-expedition problem. It does not establish temporal exposure accuracy. NASA POWER is used only for climatology, while the blended-product temporal sensitivity ends after expedition 4 and covers a different interval. A comparison on matched observations and an independent suitable precipitation or moisture product is needed before treating the surface/root 90-day signals as robust environmental responses.

Five shared expeditions also provide limited temporal replication. Fourteen of 35 repeatedly sampled bulk sites have only two visits, and only four have all five. The 60-site ERA5 record contains only 41 distinct daily series. Site resampling alone does not address shared weather errors or residual spatial dependence. Report leave-expedition-out stability, climate-grid/spatial sensitivity and bootstrap diagnostics. The current conditional associations can be informative without establishing general interannual responses.

The equivalence claims additionally concern `log2(GE + 0.01)`, not unmodified copies per genome. Group 2a is at or below that pseudocount in 176/546 samples. A ±0.5 bound on this transformed scale can conceal a materially larger proportional change near zero. Justify the biological bound and test its sensitivity to the transformation.

**The revised PERMANOVA still uses the wrong exchangeability unit (R2-18).** `how(blocks=site)` allows individual samples to exchange within a site across expeditions and positions. It does not preserve site-visit clusters. The primary dataset contains 60 position/visit cells with two replicates and 38 with three. A whole-visit permutation design, or an appropriately aggregated analysis preserving position structure, is required. The dispersion test also uses unrestricted permutations. Reporting dispersion is helpful, but does not repair the permutation design.

**Public reproducibility remains unfinished (R2-25).** The frozen tables make this audit possible, and the targeted IBEX provenance checks succeeded. Nevertheless, the advertised retrieval requires institutional credentials, upstream typing and some new input derivations are not supplied through a complete local entry point, and P3 claims remain outside the verification harness. The response’s assertion that all upstream scripts were copied is not borne out by `lifeonair/code/`. Supply the public archive, explicit dependencies, deterministic seeds and an executable P3 verification route before submission.

**Minor issues**

**Annotation reconciliation needs to be completed (R2-06–07).** The authoritative-looking `atmo_classification_v2.tsv` still labels 1,619 retained AYRGAGR entries as form I. The active analyses avoid this through separate corrected files, but downstream reuse is unsafe. Neighbourhood summaries also retain removed-contig loci: they count 1,124 group-1l proteins rather than the 1,111 retained proteins. Report consistent retained-locus denominators.

Absence of either searched CoxL motif is also not equivalent to a missing motif region. Two motif-less form-I calls are 790 and 810 residues long with >98% query coverage. Inspect the aligned active-site region, and distinguish truncation, sequence divergence and alternative function rather than treating all motif-less calls as truncated proteins.

**The DNA result should remain a conditional association (R2-13–14).** The reduction from −2.120 to −1.361 is a 35.8% attenuation of a regression coefficient, not a demonstrated biomass-mediated fraction. Nor does unequal behaviour among groups exclude compositional expansion: some guilds can increase alongside total biomass while others increase less. The baseline DNA model is useful, but the “single-kit visits” sensitivity drops kit adjustment across visits, and the bulk-only sensitivity omits the visit intercept. Preserve those distinctions and report cohort composition and diagnostics.

**Several new analyses need more precise estimand and method descriptions (R2-19–21).** The root decomposition’s −0.00465 is an abundance-weighted contribution to community carrier share, not a direct change in an average within-genus carrier fraction. Its turnover proportion is reproducible; my site bootstrap gives approximately 96.7–98.3%, conditional on the catalogue and decomposition convention. Describe an accounting decomposition rather than a demonstrated biological mechanism.

The mixed-genus precipitation model is a weighted Gaussian `lmer` on clipped logit fractions, not the binomial mixed model stated in Methods. Likewise, the CBB analysis is a three-KO co-occurrence screen. It does not validate RuBisCO form, catalytic competence, pathway completeness or missing-gene uncertainty. The reported 18%/8% should be labelled candidate co-carriage unless those checks are supplied.

**Multiplicity and null-result wording remain uneven (R2-22–23).** The code adjusts position tests separately by contrast and temporal tests separately by exposure; the manuscript’s broad family descriptions do not identify these boundaries. New CBB and mechanism tests also need a declared inferential status. Report all relevant estimates and intervals. Replace “changed no more than controls” with “no additional change was detected relative to controls,” unless a justified equivalence analysis is supplied. Raw root/bulk CoxL is significantly negative in the saved contrast table.

**The literature comparison needs one targeted addition and a more differentiated carbon interpretation (R2-24).** Li and López are now treated substantially more accurately. However, the new genome-trait section omits Wang et al. (2025), whose publisher abstract explicitly connects aridity, trace-gas use, genome size and predicted growth across 374 MAGs. The full text should be obtained for a focused comparison; I could verify the publisher abstract but not retrieve the complete article. [Wang et al., *Global Change Biology*](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/gcb.70498)

The supplied Baril 2023 XML reports carbon-dependent responses of group-1h markers, and Kropp 2026 concerns glycerol-specific regulation of Huc, group 2a. These papers make carbon response more specific to substrate and physiological context; they should not be represented as evidence for a uniquely group-1l mechanism. Their digest summaries should not substitute for their actual findings.

**Figures and remaining wording need a final evidence-based pass (R2-26–28).** Figure 1 still contains text at about 4.1 pt in the compiled manuscript. Trees lack identifying tips and useful support annotation. Figure 2c combines different cohorts and estimands under “after adjustments”; label sample/site counts and interval methods for each row.

The assertion that controls “did not scale with DNA” conflicts with their estimated slopes and standard errors; their associations are smaller, not absent. The copy-number change is −0.185, rather than −0.20 to two decimals, and uses 105 paired visits. CoxL surface residual Moran’s test is \(p=0.048\), contradicting “but not for the other groups.” Qualify the remaining realised-growth wording.

Finally, 95.8% of the surface temperature predictor’s variance is explained by expedition. Its reported positive coefficient means that depletion weakens with higher temperature; it does not directly show heat-induced depletion. Describe the residual exposure contrast, its observational limitations and its stability before using it to motivate a surface-filter mechanism.

**Part 2 — Findings**

**Part 1 — Review**

The manuscript titled “Group 1l hydrogen scavengers are relatively depleted beside plant roots across the hyperarid Rub’ al-Khali” analyses 546 metagenomes from 59 sites using a 5,438-genome catalogue and read-based marker quantification. Its principal result is a root/bulk group-1l contrast of −2.275 log₂ across 106 visits at 47 sites, with an excess over reference markers of −1.827. New analyses relate this pattern to DNA concentration, genus composition and predicted growth potential, and examine surface and precipitation associations. The replicated, group-resolved relative root contrast remains the strongest contribution. The revision substantially improves the biological framing, but several new analyses contain implementation errors or support narrower conclusions than those presented.

I recommend **major revision**. The central relative-abundance observation survives independent recalculation. However, duplicated spatial records, incorrect handling of absent genera, overstated benchmark separation, incomplete contamination sensitivity and unsupported precipitation-robustness claims require correction. These are consequential problems in the revised evidence, not reasons to reopen the PI’s decisions about relative depletion, the narrowed root/surface comparison or exploratory framing.

I independently reaggregated all 1,716 usable sample-by-threshold records from the archived read counts: the maximum GE discrepancy was \(4.99994\times10^{-6}\), consistent with output rounding. All 573 screening metadata records specified 100-nt truncation and a 25-aa minimum alignment. ERA5 climatologies and antecedent sums also reproduced to numerical precision. Benchmark, genome-size and corrected-growth outputs matched their IBEX copies; the recorded jobs were 53613713, 53611804 and 53614016. I did not rerun DIAMOND, phylogenetic inference or the complete R workflows. The micromamba wrapper attempted temporary-file creation and was blocked by read-only permissions; subsequent recalculations used in-memory Python. No files were modified.

The PDF integrity scans detected no reviewer-directed instructions. Flags concerned small scientific labels, publisher marks such as `1234567890();,:`, and production headers. Figure typography remains a substantive presentation issue.

**Round-1 dispositions**

“Resolved” includes an accepted withdrawal or appropriately narrowed claim; it does not imply that the underlying biological uncertainty has disappeared.
